Cranfield's assumptions are enumerable, and a knowledge-context system violates several by construction: the collection and information needs are fixed; every document is labelled for every query; relevance is topical similarity; a document's relevance is independent of any other document's; there is one query answered by one ranked list; judgments represent the user population [96]. **[strong]** Per-user permission filtering breaks "fixed collection"; representing conflict or redundancy breaks independence [97]; content churn breaks "fixed"; a context assembled for an agent rather than a ranked list for a person breaks the single-list assumption. Each violation kc keeps must be paired with either a metric that models it or an explicit statement that the number does not cover it.

Four load-bearing facts about pooled judgments:

**[strong] Pooling finds 50–70% of the relevant documents, and the shortfall clusters.** Zobel estimated that increasing pool depth from 100 to 200 on TREC 3/4/5 would yield ~35 more relevant documents per query (+32%), with ~85% of those coming from the 10 topics that already had the most answers; he concluded "the assumption that unjudged documents are irrelevant is not well founded" and that "measures based on recall are highly uncertain" [98]. Do not report recall, MAP or R@k from a pooled in-house collection and call it coverage.

**[strong] Pool bias is conservative, which makes it a false-negative machine rather than a false-positive one.** Buckley et al. show a constant-size pool over-represents documents containing topic *title words*, with the effect depending on collection size; one TREC 2005 AQUAINT run showed a 23% leave-one-out difference [99]. But because unjudged relevant documents are scored non-relevant, the bias only ever *depresses* the affected system's score. The cost is precise: "a system based on the presence of semantic concepts instead of the presence of specific terms may retrieve a high proportion of unjudged documents which are relevant but which do not contain title words, and thus have a lower evaluation score than warranted." If kc pools qrels from its own current retrievers, its existing stack keeps looking fine and any architecturally novel retriever is systematically under-credited — a failure nobody would notice.

**[strong] The likely failure mode for a knowledge base is the opposite of sparse labels: too many relevants.** Voorhees, Craswell and Lin report from TREC Deep Learning 2021 that judgment budgets were exhausted before an adequate sample of the relevant set could be found — the collection is not reusable, recall measures are unreliable *even for pool contributors*, early-precision measures cannot discriminate because the maximum is easily reached, and the standard collection-quality tools also fail because they all depend on system scores [100]. In an organisational knowledge base, a typical question probably has many acceptable supporting passages. If most topics have more than ~10 acceptable answers, a graded qrel set is the wrong instrument and pairwise preference judgments over the top of the ranking are the right one.

**[strong] Single-gold-answer labels fail in the other direction and mis-rank exactly at the top.** 94% of MS MARCO's ~6,980 passage dev queries have exactly one known relevant passage; crowd workers making pairwise preference judgments preferred a modern neural ranker's top result to the labelled passage more often than not — the ranker is "better than perfect" under MRR. Preference-derived qrels won 91.8% of their pairings against 46.6% for the originals, and re-scoring 16 leaderboard runs gave Kendall's τ = 0.65 with dramatic reordering at the top [101]. The cheap, obvious design — label "the right chunk" per question — will be wrong within one or two model generations, and wrong precisely where kc cares.

### Sizing, testing, and the shelf life of a collection

| Decision | What the evidence says | Strength |
|---|---|---|
| How many topics | Voorhees & Buckley: ~8–9% **absolute** MAP difference needed at 25 topics for the ordering to hold on fresh topics 95% of the time, ~5–6% at 50 [102]. Sanderson & Zobel show that method inflates the error; with resampling, 15–20% **relative** MAP suffices at 50 topics — but below 25 topics, significance still does not guarantee the result repeats [103] | strong |
| Realistic power | Urbano et al., simulating with a known null over 500M p-values: with AP at 50 topics and a realistic δ=0.01, only **9.47%** of comparisons come up significant, and 7.3% of those are Type III (right rejection, wrong direction) [104] | strong |
| Which test | Paired t-test or permutation. Urbano et al. — the only study with control over the true null — find both hold nominal α, while **Wilcoxon and sign have inflated Type I error that worsens as the topic set grows** [104]; Smucker et al. independently conclude their "use should be discontinued" [105] | strong — overturns Zobel's own 1998 advice [98] |
| Reusing the collection | Carterette: once the multiple hypotheses actually tested against a shared collection are modelled, p-values rise "by orders of magnitude" and "it is very difficult to conclude that anything is significant" [106]. Fuhr adds that reuse is *sequential* multiple testing, worse than simultaneous, and honest correction would count every unpublished test too [107] | strong |
| How many judges per document | **One competent judge is enough for relative comparisons.** Voorhees: assessor overlap ~0.36, Kendall's τ = 0.890 for MAP across judgment sets [108]; Bailey et al.: low agreement between gold/silver/bronze judges, "consistent but small" differences in rankings [109]; Parry et al. reproduced it in the neural setting where overlap fell to **0.15** and τ still came out 0.897 [110] | strong — three replications across 25 years |
| …but what to screen for | Not agreement — **systematic error**. Carterette & Soboroff simulated assessors making systematic errors and found average scores robust while "assessor errors can have a large effect on system rankings" [111]. This is why an LLM judge is dangerous in a way a sloppy human judge is not: its mistakes correlate with the retrieval model's mistakes | moderate — simulation study |
| Building it cheaply | Interactive Searching and Judging, explicitly designed so "a small research team [can] develop an effective test collection using minimal resources" [112]; Move-to-Front / variable-depth pooling (+66% relevants found for the same effort on TREC-5 [98]); depth-10 pools cost 11–14% of depth-100 while surfacing 1.7–3.6× more relevants **per unit of effort**, and the saved budget buys 350–450 topics instead of 50 [103] | strong — with one open caveat: shallow-pool reusability for non-contributing systems was flagged untested in 2005 and is still unsettled |
| Shelf life | Parry et al.: today's best rerankers (0.78–0.80 nDCG@10 on DL'19) sit **inside** the band their aggregated human annotators achieve (0.76–0.81), so further reported gains above ~0.81 "may not indicate that a system is better than another in a meaningful way" [110]. Keller et al.: as documents and qrels evolve, both absolute *and relative* system effectiveness change substantially [113] | moderate — the temporal study is a preprint |
| Comparing your number to anyone else's | You cannot, raw. AP/nDCG/P@d scores "do not allow the performance of a system tested on one collection to be readily compared with that of a system tested on a different collection", and ideal-ranking normalisation corrects this "with quite limited success"; score standardisation against a reference set of systems does work [114] | moderate |

Two ceilings bound everything above. **[strong]** Offline gains do not reliably transfer to user task performance: Hersh, Turpin et al. deliberately picked the term-weighting scheme with the largest batch improvement and found it "did not" help real users on an instance-recall task [115], and Sakai's SERP-preference data (1,127 triplets, 15 assessors each) puts the metric–user agreement ceiling at **0.7424 for nDCG, 0.7052 for AP** — statistically indistinguishable from plain Precision at 0.7054 [116]. Even the best offline metric disagrees with roughly one user in four. **[strong]** And Soboroff's ideal-ranking argument sets a harder ceiling: the ranking that places all judged-relevant documents ahead of all others maximises any standard metric, so **whatever writes the answer key caps measurable performance** [96]. A system that finds genuinely relevant documents absent from the qrels is necessarily scored below perfect. The escape hatch is a *privileged* judge — one holding information the retrieval systems lack, such as a model trained on existing human labels for these topics.

**[strong]** If kc has live users, the arithmetic changes decisively. Chapelle et al., across Bing, Yahoo! and a scientific-literature engine, found interleaving agreed with offline nDCG/MAP on direction whenever the offline test was significant, *and* found significant preferences where expert judgments could not — one manually judged query is worth about **ten** interleaved clicked queries [117]. Paired online comparison is the only evidence that speaks to satisfaction rather than to a proxy; the judged set is for regression testing and pre-launch screening.

One caveat that bears directly on any permission-filtered system. **[moderate]** Sanderson et al. found that "the relative performance of retrieval systems when evaluated on one part of a test collection may bear little or no similarity to the relative performance measured on a different part", strongly enough to flip statistically significant comparisons [118]. If each kc user's effective corpus is a permission-filtered sub-collection, a single global ranking of retrieval configurations is not warranted by this literature. Evaluate on the broadest realistic sub-corpus *and* at least one restrictive one.

### RAG evaluation: what the judge can and cannot adjudicate

The most actionable distinction in this whole literature is the level at which automatic evaluation works. **[strong]** TREC 2024 RAG-track meta-evaluation found automatic nugget evaluation agrees strongly with manual human nugget evaluation **at the run (system) level**, while per-topic instance-level agreement is explicitly flagged as needing further research [119] — mirroring the classic IR result [108]. Automatic metrics are adequate for *ranking configurations against each other* and inadequate for *adjudicating individual answers*. Never surface a judge score as a per-answer quality signal to users or to routing logic.

Everything else is a constraint on how far even the run-level use can be pushed:

- **[strong] The headline judge–human agreement number is not chance-corrected.** Across 21 judges from 9 providers, 3 benchmarks and ~541,000 judgments, the gap between exact-match agreement and Cohen's κ is **33–41 percentage points** on MT-Bench, and judge rankings shift by up to 14 positions across benchmarks [120]. The famous "GPT-4 achieves over 80% agreement, the same level as between humans" [121] is an exact-match figure of exactly this kind. *(Single preprint, abstract read only — treat the 33–41 range as needing verification, the direction as sound.)*
- **[strong] Judges agree with domain experts only on questions they can answer themselves.** On BFF-Bench (160 expert-authored questions, 1,200 expert correctness judgments), reference-free judging degrades sharply beyond the judge's own competence; supplying expert-written reference answers largely fixes it [122]. This is the decisive argument against reference-free evaluation for kc, whose entire purpose is questions the base model cannot answer.
- **[strong] Agreement at the top of the leaderboard collapses — which is the only place it matters.** Clarke and Dietz reproduce an overall τ = 0.89 between manual and automatic assessment on TREC RAG 2024, then show it falls to **0.51 among the top 20 systems and 0.56 among the top 15**; a run built deliberately to exploit the automatic judge placed 5th automatic and 28th manual; and simulating circularity by using the same LLM as final-stage reranker *and* assessor drove τ to 0.38 at top-10 and **−0.40 at top-5**, with twelve systems scoring above 0.95 nDCG under the LLM while scoring 0.68–0.72 under humans [123].
- **[strong] Position bias is the dominant judging bias, and reproducibility is not validity.** Reordering alone made Vicuna-13B "beat" ChatGPT on 66 of 80 queries [124]; across 15 judges and >150,000 instances position bias is systematic and only weakly related to length [125]. Two production-deployed judges showed test–retest reliability >0.95 coexisting with position bias >0.10 — a "consistency–bias paradox" [120]. Present both orders and average, or score pointwise with randomised rubric order.
- **[moderate] Judges are agreeable: measured TPR ~96% but TNR <25%** on code-feedback evaluation [126], corroborated by overrating behaviour in relevance assessment with high sensitivity to passage length and surface lexical cues [127]. Combined with class imbalance this yields high headline accuracy while catching almost no failures. Measure the judge's true-negative rate on deliberately seeded bad outputs; a gate that never fires manufactures confidence.
- **[strong] Correlation figures are measured over the pairs you do not need to resolve.** Restricting correlation to the small score differences actually observed in practice, the best estimate of ROUGE's correlation with human judgment is near zero [128][129]. A metric advertised at "0.85 correlation with humans" was likely measured over easy, far-apart system pairs. Estimate correlation restricted to your target effect size — typically 1–3 points — and report a confidence interval.
- **[moderate] Judges are not deterministic at temperature 0**, with completeness scoring least stable [130]. *(Single preprint; no variance magnitudes reported, so the repeated-sampling budget this implies cannot be derived from the literature.)*
- **[moderate] Preference leakage is a distinct, harder-to-detect contamination channel**: judges favour "student" models trained on synthetic data from a related model, across same-model, inheritance and same-family relatedness [131]. Judge selection must consider the generator's *training-data lineage*, not just its name.
- **[moderate] Forced-choice validation selects bad judges.** Under rating indeterminacy — items where multiple ratings are legitimately valid — standard forced-choice validation selects judges performing **up to 31% worse** than approaches modelling multi-label response sets [132]; and the majority of human annotator disagreement stems from task underspecification and response style, not noise [133]. Your rubric must permit "both acceptable" and "question underspecified".

On benchmarks themselves: **[strong]** a systematic review of 445 benchmarks from leading NLP/ML venues by 29 expert reviewers found field-wide patterns in measured phenomena, tasks and scoring metrics that undermine the validity of the resulting claims [134]. **[strong]** Contamination is real, measurable, benchmark-specific and wildly variable — 1%–45% across 15+ models and six benchmarks, producing accuracy boosts up to 14% (C-Eval) and 7% (HellaSwag) but negligible gains on MMLU [135] — and per-item detection is unreliable because "current LLMs might learn data distributions rather than memorizing individual instances" [136]. The cheap, universal mitigation is a **closed-book control arm**: the no-retrieval score is the floor kc must beat, and the oracle-context score is the ceiling that localises whether a failure is retrieval or generation [137].

Two findings bear directly on how much any single published RAG design claim should move kc's architecture. **[strong]** The SIGIR 2024 "Power of Noise" result — random documents improving accuracy by up to 35% — was directly reproduced and then shown to "appear, weaken, or disappear under small changes to prompt formulation and decoding limits", with substantial contributions from truncation and malformed generations; the reproducers conclude the effect "cannot be robustly confirmed as a general benefit of noisy retrieval" [138][139]. **[moderate]** Position effects fare no better: a systematic reproducibility study finds findings "remain inconsistent and hard to reproduce across models, datasets, and evaluation protocols", identifies **topic sampling** as a major variance source that can mask *or* exaggerate ordering effects, and warns that "conclusions drawn from idealised setups do not always transfer to real-world RAG pipelines" [140][141].

### The minimum honest in-house evaluation

Synthesising the construction literature [98][103][112] with the RAG-specific constraints, this is the smallest harness that licenses a real claim. Each line is there because something above says the cheaper version fails silently.

1. **At least 50 topics, drawn from observed user queries, not invented.** Below 25, a significance test buys nothing [103]; at 50 you are powered for large architectural differences, not prompt tweaks [104]. Practitioner evidence says the queries must be *novel observed* ones — a production team reports "common RAG benchmark evaluation techniques have not been useful for evaluating responses to novel user questions" [142]. *(Single-organisation evidence.)*
2. **Judgments pooled shallow (depth ~10) from deliberately diverse retrievers** — lexical, dense, and at least one interactive human search — with variable depth on the topics that keep yielding [98][103][112]. Pooling from your current stack alone bakes in the term-overlap bias that will under-credit your next architecture [99].
3. **One competent judge per topic, screened for systematic bias rather than for agreement** [108][109][110][111]. Spend the saved budget on more topics.
4. **A top-heavy graded metric (nDCG@k or RBP), never recall or MAP, on a pooled collection** [98][116]. Add a redundancy-discounted measure if kc must surface complementary or conflicting sources, because nDCG rewards five copies of one claim over one claim plus its counter-evidence [97].
5. **Four mandatory arms in every comparison: closed-book, oracle-context, tuned hybrid BM25, and your system** [137][143]. If kc does not beat tuned hybrid lexical retrieval on kc's own corpus, it is not yet shown to work — regardless of the embedding vendor's benchmark. Note that the baseline must be BM25+RM3-class, not default BM25: four of five neural rerankers failed to significantly beat a tuned BM25+RM3 on Robust04 [144].
6. **Paired t-test or permutation test on per-topic scores, with effect size and CI**; never Wilcoxon or sign [104][105].
7. **A pre-registered comparison list and a held-out topic set touched only at release decisions.** Day-to-day runs are a development signal explicitly labelled *not a claim* [106][107].
8. **An LLM judge over the full set, a stratified human-labelled subsample (order 200–500 items) per release, and prediction-powered inference intervals as the reported number** [145][146][147]. PPI gives valid confidence intervals with no assumptions about the automatic labeller, which turns "how much human labelling do we need" into a tunable parameter rather than an argument.
9. **Judge hygiene as config, not convention**: judge from a different provider lineage than both the generator and its training-data source [131]; versioned prompt (paraphrase alone moved κ by 0.2 in Bing's deployment [148]); reported chance-corrected agreement plus class balance [120]; measured true-negative rate on seeded failures [126]; repeated sampling with a variance estimate [130].
10. **Retrieval success and abstention instrumented as separate annotated outcomes**, not folded into one blended RAG score — a Bayesian decomposition across 27 configurations shows "substantial behavioral differences between systems that appear equivalent under marginal metrics", and finds retrieval-success annotations *more informative than task-success annotations* for scarce budget [149]. *(Preprint.)* Prompt-based abstention is not abstention: models instructed to refuse when evidence is absent still answered 41.6% of questions containing misleading information, because "prompt-based abstention asks whether context is sufficient, not whether it is correct" [150]. *(Single preprint.)*
11. **Periodic re-judging budgeted from the start**, with a stopping rule: once the top configurations sit inside the band of your own human judges' agreement, the collection has stopped measuring [110][113].

**What kc may legitimately claim, and what it may not.**

| Permitted | Not permitted | Why |
|---|---|---|
| "Configuration A ranks known-relevant context higher than B on our topic set, δ = x, p = y, CI [a,b]" | "A is better for users" | Metric–user agreement ceiling ≈ 0.74 [116]; batch improvements demonstrated not to transfer [115] |
| "A beats a tuned BM25+RM3 baseline on our corpus" | "A beats BM25" (citing a paper's BM25 number) | Published BM25 baselines are systematically understated [151]; 60% of 109 Robust04 papers reported *best* results below an untuned RM3 run [144] |
| "Our nDCG@10 on our topics is x" as an internal trend | Comparing that x to any published figure | Scores are not comparable across collections without standardisation [114] |
| "The judge agrees with our human labels at κ = x on this class balance" | "Our judge is 85% accurate, comparable to humans" | Raw exact-match inflates by 33–41 points [120][152] |
| "We detected no regression on the fixed query set this release" | "This release is better" (from the same set, run daily) | Collection reuse is sequential multiple testing [106][107] |
| "Retrieval recall on our seeded known-item set is x" | "We find everything relevant" | A live full-text legal system retrieved under 20% of relevant documents against lawyers' stipulated 75% requirement [59] |

The defensible bottom line: an in-house evaluation of this shape can honestly support a **relative, corpus-specific, effect-sized claim about ranking**, screened offline and confirmed online where users exist. It cannot support an absolute quality number, a claim about user benefit, a comparison to published results, or a claim that any particular retrieval architecture is "the right one" for corpora other than kc's own.

### What the evidence does not support

**"Dense/vector search has superseded keyword search; BM25 is a legacy baseline."** In BEIR's zero-shot evaluation every dense retriever tested averaged *below* BM25 across 18 datasets: DPR −47.7%, ANCE −7.4%, GenQ −3.6%, TAS-B −2.8% nDCG@10; learned sparse term-weighting was worst of all (DeepCT −27.9%) [85]. In the 2024 reproduction with much stronger models the authors still conclude that "none of the models are viable as a replacement [for BM25] yet, given that there are clearly situations where effectiveness is substantially lower" [88].

**"BEIR proves BM25 beats dense retrieval out of domain."** The disconfirming result is itself partly disconfirmed, and this must be stated whenever the previous point is. BEIR's own hole@10 analysis — the fraction of a system's top-10 that no assessor ever judged — is 6.4% for BM25 and 2.8% for docT5query but 31.8% for TAS-B, 30.6% for DPR and 14.4% for ANCE. After manually judging 980 missing TREC-COVID pairs, BM25 moved .656→.668 while ANCE moved .654→**.735**, from just below BM25 to 6.7 points above it [85]. An independent reproducibility study of Touché-2020 found a short-passage artifact plus large numbers of unjudged results; after denoising, neural effectiveness improved "by up to 0.52 in nDCG@10" (BM25 still won, but by far less) [86]. Only 2 of 18 BEIR datasets have been re-judged, so **the true size of BM25's advantage is unknown**.

**"Hybrid retrieval reliably gives a meaningful accuracy lift."** A random-effects meta-analysis across 29 BEIR test sets found a score-averaging hybrid of the two strongest components significantly better than SPLADE alone in only **14 of 29** cases and than Contriever alone in **16 of 29**, with summary effects of 0.01 [0.01, 0.02] and 0.02 [0.02, 0.03] nDCG@10 — and significantly *worse* on some sets (SPLADE vs hybrid on BioASQ: −0.04 [−0.06, −0.02]) [88]. Luo et al. separately found hybrid added value only in the large-corpus setting, not the small one [94]. Hybrid buys robustness and variance reduction, not accuracy.

**"Reciprocal Rank Fusion is the safe, parameter-free, tuning-free default."** It is none of those. RRF is parametric with one *more* free parameter than a convex combination; its NDCG "swings wildly" as a function of those parameters; a tuned RRF generalises poorly out-of-domain, with the sign of the useful asymmetry reversing between in-domain and out-of-domain data; and a convex combination of normalised scores beats it on all nine datasets tested at p<0.01 (MS MARCO .454 vs .425, FiQA .496 vs .464, SciFact .753 vs .730) [90]. Because RRF is a function of ranks alone it discards the score distribution and is not Lipschitz continuous. Yang et al. independently found that fusing on ranks while ignoring retrieval scores made effectiveness "significantly lower" [144]. The literature is genuinely split — Chen et al. recommended RRF for exactly the zero-shot case [91] — so measure rather than adopt either default.

**PROVENANCE FAILURE — "the RRF constant k=60 is a principled, empirically-derived value."** It traces to a single sentence in the original SIGIR 2009 short paper: "k = 60 was fixed during a pilot investigation and not altered during subsequent validation" [153]. The pilot sweep was flat (MAP .2134 at k=20 through .2147 at k=80) and the authors say the choice "was not critical" — in *their* setting, which was fusing ~30 configurations of one engine plus full sets of TREC-submitted runs, not two lists. It was never validated on two-way lexical+dense fusion, and Bruch et al. find substantial parameter sensitivity in exactly that setting [90]. There is no primary source for k=60 being right for hybrid search.

**"Dense retrievers fail on our data because of domain shift, so more in-domain training data will fix it."** Tested directly and refuted. On LIMIT, fine-tuning an off-the-shelf embedder on an in-domain *training* split moved recall@10 from near zero to 2.8 — essentially nothing — while fine-tuning on the *test* split solved it, showing the models can only memorise [89]. The limitation is dimensional and provable, C(n,k) ≤ (1+1/γ)^d, and it bit on a corpus of **46 documents** with queries as simple as "who likes Apples?".

**"Small corpora are the easy case — with a few thousand documents any embedding model will find the right thing."** The most direct evidence points the opposite way. On LIMIT-small (46 documents, 1000 trivially-worded queries), models at 1024–4096 dimensions score recall@2 between 19.0% and 54.3% and cannot solve it even at recall@20, while BM25 reaches 97.8%; 12 dimensions suffice to encode that configuration under free optimisation, yet real 64-dimension models still fail, so "real models perform significantly worse than the bounds" [89]. Small corpus size shrinks the theoretical requirement without making real models succeed.

**"Choosing the right BM25 implementation matters a lot."** Eight BM25 scoring variants across three TREC collections with the indexing pipeline held fixed showed **no statistically significant differences** between any variant on any collection (ANOVA + Tukey's HSD; Robust04 AP spanned .2516–.2571). The authors note "differences due to more mundane settings (such as the choice of stopwords) are often larger" [92]. The claim is right for the wrong reason: the *analysis chain* is where the 10-point swings live [88].

**"Adding a neural reranking stage on top of your pipeline will give you the gain that paper reported."** Gains are frequently non-additive. Five neural rerankers stacked on a well-tuned BM25+RM3 baseline on Robust04 produced exactly one statistically significant improvement (DRMM, .3033 → .3152, p=0.0032); DSSM, CDSSM, KNRM and DUET did not improve on the baseline at all [144]. The general result, first reported by Armstrong et al. in 2009, is that improvements measured over weak baselines do not survive being applied to a strong one [154].

**"Neural/LLM retrieval has delivered large measurable improvements over classical IR."** Armstrong et al. score-standardised every TREC ad hoc and Robust submission from 1994 to 2005 against a common reference set and found **no upward trend** in median or top-end effectiveness [154]. Across 109 Robust04 papers, 33% used a baseline below the TREC median, 60% reported *best* results below an untuned open-source BM25+RM3 run, and only 5.5% exceeded the best 2004 TREC submission [144]. This is a claim about the evidence, not about the technology.

**PROVENANCE FAILURE — "a similarity score above 0.8 means the result is relevant."** No source establishes this for any model. Steck, Ekanadham and Kallus show analytically that cosine similarity of learned embeddings "can yield arbitrary and therefore meaningless similarities" — in some regularised linear models the similarities are not even unique, and the values depend on regularisation choices made for entirely unrelated reasons; deep models carry analogous implicit regularisation [93]. They explicitly caution against using it blindly. Every fixed threshold in circulation is folklore.

**"There is a best embedding model; pick the leaderboard leader."** MTEB evaluated 33 models over 58 datasets and 8 task types and concluded "no particular text embedding method dominates across all tasks" [155]. Leaderboard rankings are additionally unstable to prompt choice [156], and MS MARCO leaderboard improvements "are almost never qualified with significance testing" [157].

**PROVENANCE FAILURE — "GPT-4-class judges agree with humans about as well as humans agree with each other (~80%), so LLM-as-judge is a validated substitute."** The 80% figure is *uncorrected exact-match agreement* [121]. On the same benchmark, chance-corrected Cohen's κ is 33–41 percentage points lower across 21 judges and ~541,000 judgments [120]. JUDGE-BENCH (20 NLP datasets, 11 LLMs) finds substantial variance by property, human-expertise level and whether the text is human- or model-generated, and concludes LLMs "should be carefully validated against human judgments before being used as evaluators" [152]. Nobody has published a chance-corrected number supporting the popular claim.

**PROVENANCE FAILURE — "RAGAS faithfulness/relevance scores are good enough to gate a release or compare vendors."** RAGAS's entire published validation is **50 questions from 50 Wikipedia pages, 2 annotators**, reported as pairwise-preference accuracy (faithfulness 0.95, answer relevance 0.78, context relevance 0.70) — never as calibrated absolute scores, and never off Wikipedia [158][159]. No source anywhere establishes that a threshold like "faithfulness > 0.9" means anything. Four independent replications in telecom, travel, academic-thesis and business-data settings report poor or absent alignment with human scores, one stating flatly that "quantitative and Ragas metrics do not align with human evaluation" and another that "RAGAs based faithfulness shows limited reliability in this setup" [160][161][162][163]. *(Four small field studies, consistent direction, none large.)* Reference-free judging is additionally worst precisely where a knowledge-context system operates: questions the judge cannot answer itself [122].

**"An automatic judge with high Kendall's τ against human judgments can replace human judgments."** The high τ is measured over the comparisons nobody needs. Clarke and Dietz reproduce τ = 0.89 overall on TREC RAG 2024, then show it drops to 0.51 among the top 20 systems and 0.56 among the top 15 — and excluding merely the weakest 15 of 75 systems already drops it to 0.84, showing the headline was inflated by easy-to-distinguish poor systems [123]. A run built to exploit the judge placed 5th automatic and 28th manual [123], and deliberately targeting a nugget-based judge achieves near-perfect scores when prompt templates or gold nuggets leak or are predictable [164]. Soboroff's ideal-ranking argument makes it structural: an LLM-written answer key reduces the evaluation to "how close is this system to that model" [96].

**"Verbosity/length bias is the dominant failure of LLM judges."** Under a single pairwise rubric across 21 judges, measured verbosity bias was <0.011 while position bias exceeded 0.10 in two production judges [120]; a systematic bias-mitigation study finds *style* bias dominant, "far exceeding position bias" [165]; and length-controlling AlpacaEval — the canonical debiasing intervention — moves Spearman correlation with Chatbot Arena only from 0.94 to 0.98, meaning the uncontrolled metric was already highly rank-correlated at system level [166]. Length bias is real but neither dominant nor the main threat to system-level rankings.

**"Self-preference is a universal, first-order failure of LLM-as-judge."** Established in open-ended generation with a demonstrated causal link to self-recognition [167], but a systematic study across four extractive QA datasets, multiple model families and many prompt variations reports "no self-preference bias, even when the same model serves as both QA model and judge" [168]. The genuinely worrying variant is preference *leakage* — bias toward models trained on synthetic data from a related model — which its authors call "harder to detect compared to previously identified biases" [131]. **[contested]**

**"Adding irrelevant or random documents to the context improves RAG accuracy (up to +35%)."** A direct reproduction confirms the effect under the original setup — earlier-generation LLMs, restrictive prompting, constrained decoding — then shows it "can appear, weaken, or disappear under small changes to prompt formulation and decoding limits", with substantial contributions from truncation and malformed generations, concluding the effect "cannot be robustly confirmed as a general benefit of noisy retrieval under these experimental conditions" [138][139].

**"'Lost in the middle' is settled, so put the best evidence first or last."** A systematic reproducibility study finds position-effect findings "remain inconsistent and hard to reproduce across models, datasets, and evaluation protocols", that topic sampling is a major variance source able to mask *or* exaggerate ordering effects, and that "conclusions drawn from idealised setups do not always transfer to real-world RAG pipelines" [140]; a separate study reports no consistently observed U-shaped curve across 7–8B models and finds mitigation methods sometimes make things worse [141]. The original result [169] stands for its setting, not as a general design rule.

**"Semantic/cluster-based chunking beats naive fixed-size chunking, and reranking is a reliable win."** Under-supported rather than refuted. In a controlled comparison on long structured academic theses, "cluster-based chunking did not outperform simpler strategies" (fixed-size and recursive) [162]; a separate multi-dataset study observed "only marginal performance improvements accompanied by higher response latency" from reranking [143]. Both are single studies — the point is that the widely-repeated claims rest on no stronger evidence than these do.

**PROVENANCE FAILURE — "RAG benchmarks are contaminated."** No primary source measures this. An arXiv full-text search for "retrieval augmented generation" AND "benchmark contamination" returned **zero results**; all located contamination literature concerns closed-book benchmarks [135][136]. The indirect argument (NQ/TriviaQA/HotpotQA are heavily contaminated closed-book, so a RAG system evaluated on them may be scored on parametric recall) is sound but unmeasured. State it as a hypothesis, and run the closed-book control arm anyway [137].

**"Benchmark contamination is a solved diagnostic — you can detect and exclude contaminated items."** A survey of 50 detection papers testing the assumptions behind instance-level detection concludes "current LLMs might learn data distributions rather than memorizing individual instances", undermining per-item detection [136]; and effect sizes vary wildly by benchmark (up to +14% C-Eval, +7% HellaSwag, negligible MMLU), so no single global adjustment is meaningful [135].

**PROVENANCE FAILURE — "a 5% improvement is meaningful; smaller differences are noise."** Sparck Jones's widely-repeated 5%-absolute rule of thumb has no statistical basis and is simultaneously too stringent for large topic sets and too lax for small ones. Zobel found a case where an 11-point average difference of **0.002** (0.149 → 0.151) *was* significant and another where 0.118 (0.210 → 0.328) was *not*; ~20% of differences smaller than 0.05 absolute were significant [98]. Conversely, at a 20–30% relative MAP difference only 57.3% of comparisons were significant, and a positive t-test with a relative MAP difference under 10% is arguably more likely a Type I error than a real result [103]. Effect size and significance are independent facts; both must be reported. Nothing in the literature establishes how large an improvement must be before a *user* of a knowledge-context system notices it — Fuhr's suggested P@10 difference of 0.02 is explicitly offered as an illustration, not a standard [107].

**"Use Wilcoxon or the sign test because IR score distributions are not normal."** The standard justification, and the evidence contradicts it. Urbano et al., the only study with control over the true null, found Wilcoxon and sign have inflated Type I error that gets **worse** as the topic set grows, while t and permutation hold nominal α [104]; Smucker et al. write that they "are simplified variants of the randomization test and their use should be discontinued" [105]; Sanderson and Zobel measured error rates of 16–10% (Wilcoxon) and 17–8% (sign) against 13–7% (t) [103]. This overturns Zobel's own 1998 recommendation [98], which is still repeated in tutorials.

**"A 30–50 query evaluation set is enough to tell which retriever is better."** Sakai's topic-set-size design, requiring 80% power at α=0.05 across 10 systems, gives required topic counts of ~45–60 for nDCG@10, ~121–131 for P+, and ~157–168 for nG@1 — and that is to detect a minimum detectable *range* of 0.15, an enormous effect [170]. Required n scales roughly as 1/minD², so realistic 0.02–0.03 differences are undetectable at these sizes. Urbano et al. put the empirical figure on it: at 50 topics with δ=0.01, only 9.47% of comparisons come up significant [104].

**"Inter-assessor disagreement is so high that test collections cannot be trusted."** Unsupported for *relative* comparisons, and checked three times across 25 years: Voorhees, overlap ~0.36, Kendall's τ = 0.890 [108]; Bailey et al., low agreement between expert and non-expert judges but "consistent but small" ranking differences [109]; Parry et al., overlap collapsed to 0.15 on modern short-passage 4-grade data with τ still 0.897 [110]. The correct worry is different: *systematic* assessor error does move system rankings substantially [111] — which is exactly the failure mode an LLM judge has and a sloppy human judge does not.

**"Pool bias means results from a pooled collection are untrustworthy."** Too pessimistic, and it obscures the real problem. The bias is conservative — unjudged relevant documents score as non-relevant, so an affected system is only ever penalised, and a claim that a new method beats a baseline remains valid [99]. The actual cost is a false *negative*: a semantically-different retriever may retrieve many unjudged-but-relevant documents lacking title words and score lower than warranted. The authors also caution that retrieving many unjudged documents is not itself proof of unfair evaluation.

**"More and deeper judgments per topic make a better collection."** Per unit of assessor effort, the opposite: a depth-10 pool costs 11–14% of a depth-100 pool while surfacing 1.7–3.6× more relevant documents per unit of effort, and P@10 achieves a *lower* error rate than MAP once effort is held constant; the same budget buys 350–450 topics instead of 50, and topics are the dominant source of variance [103]. The authors' own caveat stands and is still unsettled: the reusability of shallow-pool collections for non-contributing systems was untested in 2005 and no study since resolves it.

**"MRR, or a single labelled best answer per query, is a fine cheap way to track retrieval quality."** It stops working as soon as the systems get good, and it fails silently. 94% of ~6,980 MS MARCO passage dev queries have exactly one known relevant passage; crowd workers preferred a modern ranker's top result to the labelled one more often than not, making the ranker "better than perfect" under MRR; preference-derived qrels won 91.8% of pairings against the originals' 46.6%, and re-scoring 16 leaderboard runs gave τ = 0.65 with dramatic top-end reordering [101]. Fuhr adds that MRR can rank first-relevant ranks of 1, 2, 4 above 2, 2, 2 despite a worse mean rank, because E(1/R) ≠ 1/E(R) [107].

**"You can build a modest in-house eval set and use it to justify choices as you iterate."** Not without an explicit multiplicity discipline. Once the many hypotheses actually tested against a shared collection are modelled, p-values increase "by orders of magnitude" and "it is very difficult to conclude that anything is significant" [106]; reuse is *sequential* multiple testing, and the honest minimum correction would count every test previously run on that collection including the unpublished ones [107]. A small set a team hammers daily is a development signal, not evidence.

**"A test collection you build once is an asset you keep using."** It has a shelf life, and the modern ones are near the end of theirs. Today's best rerankers (0.78–0.80 nDCG@10 on DL'19) sit inside the band achieved by aggregating human annotators (0.76–0.81), so reported gains above that band "may not indicate that a system is better than another in a meaningful way" [110]; as documents and qrels evolve, both absolute *and relative* system effectiveness change substantially [113]; and TREC DL 2021 documents a collection that was not reusable from the day it was built [100].

**"Improving an offline metric means users will be better off."** Directly tested and falsified. Hersh, Turpin et al. deliberately selected the weighting scheme with the largest batch improvement and found it "did not" benefit real users on an instance-recall task [115]; follow-ups extended the negative result [171][172]. Sakai's SERP-preference data (1,127 triplets, 15 assessors each) caps agreement at 0.7424 for nDCG and 0.7052 for AP — not significantly better than plain Precision at 0.7054 [116]. Even the best offline metric disagrees with roughly a quarter of individual users' expressed preferences.

**"Users' sense that the system finds everything is evidence that it does."** Blair and Maron evaluated a live full-text legal system over roughly 350,000 pages and found it retrieving "less than 20 percent of the documents relevant to a particular search" — against the lawyers' stipulated requirement of 75% [59].

**"A strong score on a public RAG benchmark predicts production performance."** A production team reports "common RAG benchmark evaluation techniques have not been useful for evaluating responses to novel user questions", and that simple changes to how knowledge-base *content is authored* had large impact [142]; a realistic heterogeneous enterprise benchmark puts the best agentic RAG system at ~33, with retrieval identified as the main bottleneck [173]; and the 445-benchmark construct-validity review finds field-wide patterns that undermine the resulting claims [134]. *(The first two are single-organisation evidence.)*

**"Prompting the model to cite sources or refuse when evidence is absent gives reliable abstention."** Models instructed to refuse when evidence is absent still answered 41.6% of questions containing misleading information, because "prompt-based abstention asks whether context is sufficient, not whether it is correct" [150]. Abstention must be measured as its own conditional outcome, never assumed from an instruction. *(Single preprint.)*

**"RAG eliminates hallucination."** RAGTruth provides ~18,000 naturally generated RAG responses with manual annotation at both case and word level *including hallucination intensity*, and benchmarks hallucination frequency across LLMs — the corpus exists because grounded generation hallucinates [174].

## Conflict, staleness and time

**Marker convention.** Findings drawn from the bi-temporal / belief-revision topic review are marked **†**. That review did **not** receive an adversarial verification pass; its venue attributions, quotations and numeric claims have not been independently re-checked, so every † claim below is stated one grade more conservatively than its source review graded it. The knowledge-conflict review *was* adversarially verified, and where the two reviews disagree — they do, twice, on ClashEval's headline number and on truth-discovery parameter sensitivity — the verified reading wins and the disagreement is recorded rather than smoothed.

### The taxonomy: which conflicts are the store's problem

Conflict has two orthogonal axes: **locus** (where the disagreement lives) and **cause** (why it exists). Locus is canonical; cause is a design convenience with synthetic backing.

| Axis | Values | Evidence | What kc can represent |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Locus** [175][176] | context-memory (retrieved vs parametric), inter-context (two sources disagree), intra-memory (model self-inconsistency) | **Strong** as vocabulary, **not** as evidence — both surveys are preprints; authority comes from near-universal adoption, not peer review | Only **inter-context** is representable in a knowledge layer. Context-memory is a consumer-side property you can only influence; intra-memory is inside the weights and cannot be represented at all. This bounds the schema. |
| **Cause** [177] | misinformation, temporal discrepancy, semantic divergence (different sense/scope/jurisdiction) | **Moderate** — ConflictBank is 7.45M synthetic claim-evidence pairs over 12 LLMs; the partition is asserted by construction, not induced from real corpora, and the paper still carries "Under Review" ~2 years post-posting | The correct response differs per cause: *expire*, *down-weight*, *disambiguate*. A single `conflict: bool` is under-specified; a `(locus, cause)` pair is the minimum useful key. |

The practical warning inside the cause axis: telling genuine contradiction from **scope difference** at index time is unestablished. Gokul et al. find contradiction-detection performance "varying significantly across different types of contradictions" — and they had to *generate* simulated contradictions because naturally-occurring ones were not available at scale [178].

### What models actually do with conflicting evidence

The popular one-liner ("LLMs over-trust retrieved context") is **contested**, and the honest statement is narrower. The 2021 encoder-QA picture it replaced — "over-reliance on memorized information" [179] — genuinely no longer describes instruction-tuned LLMs uniformly, but the reversal is not clean.

| Claim | Evidence | Grade |
|---|---|---|
| Models are receptive to coherent counter-evidence — *and* show "a strong confirmation bias when the external evidence contains some information that is consistent with their parametric memory" | Xie et al., ICLR 2024 spotlight — **both halves are in the same abstract**; the widely-circulated version quotes only the first | **Contested** (two-sided by construction) [180] |
| Which knowledge wins depends on **entity popularity**: unaugmented models remain competitive on head entities; retrieval rescues the tail | Mallen et al., ACL 2023 | Moderate–strong [137] |
| "LLMs override their own correct prior >60% of the time" | ClashEval, unreplicated preprint. The adversarial pass flags this as a **response rate to deliberately planted errors**, not a base rate — the construction creates the quantity it measures | **Weak as a number**, directionally useful [181] |
| Contradiction among sources moves model confidence "only marginally" | Chen, Zhang & Choi, EMNLP 2022 | Moderate [182] |
| Models notice *that* a conflict exists but cannot localise it or answer with distinct viewpoints | Wang et al., COLM 2024 (three desiderata: identify / pinpoint / answer distinctly); WikiContradict (253 human-annotated real Wikipedia instances, 3,500+ human judgements) — worst on **implicit** conflicts requiring reasoning; WhoQA, Findings of EMNLP 2024 (5,000 questions from same-name entity collisions) | **Strong** — three independent groups, two peer-reviewed [183][184][185] |
| Models prefer LLM-*generated* context over retrieved context even when the generated context is wrong, because generated text is more question-similar and retrieval's chunking "disrupts completeness" | Tan et al., ACL 2024 Main; single unreplicated study | Moderate [186] |
| Models "often favor conflicting evidence without justification" when two retrieved sources clash | ECon, EMNLP 2024 | Moderate †[187] |
| Ordinary RAG can fall **below** no-retrieval in the worst case | Astute RAG, ACL 2025 main — its method is "the only RAG method that achieves performance comparable to or even surpassing conventional use of LLMs under the worst-case scenario" | Moderate (one strong venue, one qualitative claim) [188] |

Three design consequences survive the downgrade. **(1)** Correctness of retrieved content is a property the store must own, because the consumer demonstrably does not filter it reliably. **(2)** Handing two contradictory chunks to a prompt and asking for a balanced answer does not work — if disagreement is to be surfaced, the *spans* and the *per-claim source set* must be computed upstream. **(3)** Any agent loop that writes its own notes back into the store creates a privileged, unverified, fluent source that will out-compete fragmented primary evidence; model-generated content needs a distinct provenance class and a down-weight [186].

Do not plan to detect conflict from generator confidence alone — but do not discard confidence either. The strongest published result on exactly this question **fuses** an evidence-side conflict signal with generator confidence and gains 7.2–33.4 points of selective accuracy in incorrect-only conditions [189] (preprint). Conflict detection over the candidate set (e.g. NLI over top-k) is the load-bearing half; confidence is a usable component, not a detector. A white-box alternative exists but is background for most designs: conflict signals are linearly detectable in the residual stream before generation, with "significantly different patterns when the model relies on contextual knowledge versus parametric knowledge" [190] — a NeurIPS 2024 *workshop* paper, the weakest peer-review tier, requiring open weights and activation access.

### Staleness: semantic relevance systematically prefers the stale document

This is the cleanest positive answer in the whole section to "should the layer model time explicitly?"

- Re-rankers show "a strong bias toward older, semantically rich documents, even when they are factually obsolete" — plausibly because older documents accrete more elaborated prose and therefore more semantic mass [191]. **Caveat: the finding and its proposed fix come from the same industry lab**, and the paper is a preprint.
- Accuracy drops up to **31%** on evolving-knowledge questions across 3 real timestamped corpora (AWS release notes, Azure changes, WHO outbreak reports) over 12 open and closed models [192]; GPT-3 "tends to return outdated answers when retrieved documents do not provide sufficient information" [193].
- Outdated content that merely *coexists* with current content actively degrades output: HoH finds it "substantially reduces response accuracy by distracting models from correct information" and "can mislead models into generating potentially harmful outputs, **even when current information is available**" †[194]; a controlled clinical study stratifying PubMed abstracts by publication year concludes "retrieval similarity alone is insufficient for reliable medical RAG" †[195].
- The sharpest second-order result: **the facts where recency matters most are the ones the consumer most resists updating**. DynamicQA (Findings of EMNLP 2024) shows temporally-dynamic facts carry more intra-memory conflict and are "harder to update with context, suggesting that retrieval-augmented generation will struggle with the most commonly adapted facts" [196].

Together these say: recency must be an **explicit signal** — a filter, a rank prior, or a validity interval — enforced at retrieval, not left to the generator to discount. "Retrieve and let relevance sort it out" systematically serves stale facts.

Expiry should be **per assertion**, not a document TTL. Zhang & Choi (EMNLP 2023) predict "how long a given fact will remain true" and show that "modeling fact duration improves calibration ... under temporal misalignment, by discarding volatile facts" [197]. A blanket 90-day TTL does the opposite of what is wanted: it evicts stable facts and retains volatile ones. An expiry date can also be *predicted* rather than recorded †[198][199], but the evidence base there is two small papers — treat a predicted expiry as a low-confidence hint that triggers review, never an automatic deletion trigger.

Time-stamping content is itself a tested intervention: jointly modelling text with its timestamp improves both memorisation and **calibration** on unseen future periods, and allows refresh without retraining †[200]; temporal misalignment effects across 8 tasks / 4 domains / 5+ years are "stronger ... than have been previously reported", and continued pretraining on newer data helps far less than finetuning on target-period data †[201]. Continuously-refreshed benchmarks exist to keep measuring this †[193][202]. Read as an argument for putting effort into the store rather than the model.

### Does recency or provenance weighting recover accuracy?

| Intervention | Result | Grade |
|---|---|---|
| Credibility as a **computed weight on attention** (CrAM, AAAI 2025) | >20% improvement under misinformation pollution on NQ/TriviaQA across three model families, beating supervised fine-tuning | Moderate [203] |
| Credibility as a **training signal** (CAG, EMNLP 2024 Main) | Works; achieved by training on credibility-transformed data, not prompting | Moderate [204] |
| Source credibility in fact-checking (CONFACT, IJCAI 2025) | "Effectively incorporating source credibility significantly enhances the ability of RAG models to resolve conflicting evidence" | Moderate [205] |
| Credibility as a **prompt-level label** | Preference for institutionally-corroborated sources exists across 13 open-weight models but "can be reversed by simply repeating information from less credible sources" | Moderate; **this is the failure mode** [206] |
| Reordering passages so the trusted/freshest one sits at a favoured position | Positional bias "is actually marginal in real scenarios"; strategies rearranging by LLM positional preference "do not perform better than random shuffling" | Moderate [207] |
| Instructing the model to abstain or say "sources disagree" | Insufficient; large models "often output incorrect answers instead of abstaining when the context is not [sufficient]" | Moderate [208][183] |
| Learned source-reliability / truth discovery | Not solved on real data; see below | Moderate-negative [209][210][211] |

Three things fall out. **Credibility must be a numeric, machine-readable field consumed by the ranker or the decoder — never free text in the chunk body.** The mechanism that works is weighting or training; annotation is what fails. **Deduplication is a correctness control, not a storage optimisation**: an index holding 40 syndicated copies of a low-credibility claim and one authoritative rebuttal produces the low-credibility answer regardless of attached trust weights, because repetition reverses the preference [206]. Count *distinct sources*, not documents; cap items per source and per side. And **credibility signals must derive from provenance metadata (who / when / where), not from text-intrinsic features**, because stylistic plausibility rather than factual support drives adoption, and style is exactly what an attacker controls [212][213][214].

On the machinery: do not build a learned source-credibility scorer as v1. The first systematic implementation-and-comparison of 12 truth-discovery algorithms exists precisely because none had been done †[209]; on two Deep Web domains chosen *because* they were believed clean and consequential, the authors found "a large amount of inconsistency ... and also some sources with quite low accuracy", and framed the paper as "is the problem solved?" — answered no †[210]; at 1.6B triples from 12 extractors, extraction noise turns out to be a qualitatively different error source from the source-conflict noise the fusion methods were built for †[211]. The defensible v1 is **count the independent sources and expose the count**; any weighting scheme must beat that on your own data before shipping. The ~15-year truth-discovery line is surveyed in [215] (itself flagged as cited-by-others rather than read in the source review). The mature idea worth borrowing is *copy detection* — Dong, Berti-Equille & Srivastava explicitly model sources copying from one another [216], which is the formal version of the syndication problem above.

### Corpus poisoning, honestly scoped

PoisonedRAG (USENIX Security 2025) achieves ~90% attack success by injecting **five malicious texts per target question** into a database of millions, and finds tested defences insufficient [217]; a 13-attack / 7-defence / 5-dataset benchmark concludes "current defense techniques fail to provide robust protection" [218]. Two scope conditions the popular retelling drops. First, **the threat model requires the attacker to know the target question in advance** — this is a targeted attack against anticipated queries, not a demonstration that a corpus can be generally degraded at five-document cost. Second, the widely-repeated figures "layered defences reduce non-adaptive success from 79% to 1%, adaptive attacks reach 62%" **trace to no locatable source** and should not be repeated (see below).

Calibrate accordingly: the demonstrated exposure is an adversary who can guess or observe your queries *and* write to your index. Where nobody outside the trust boundary can write, this is a much smaller risk than the literature's tone implies — and the control is **write-path** (who may add content, provenance recorded at ingest, per-source admission policy), with read-path filtering as a supplement.

The only defence with a formal guarantee is **isolate-then-aggregate**: RobustRAG partitions retrieved passages into disjoint groups, generates independently per group, then securely aggregates, certifying "non-trivial lower bounds on response quality ... even against an adaptive attacker with full knowledge of the defense" [219]. Treat as an option, not a requirement: still no venue ~2 years after posting, 3 datasets / 3 LLMs, and certification is query-conditional. It nonetheless constrains an API shape decided early — passages must be exposable as k independently-groupable units with stable identity, i.e. return *partitioned evidence groups*, not one concatenated blob. Unresolved: whether that certification survives a corpus with genuine, non-adversarial 50/50 disagreement, which violates its implicit "most groups are clean" assumption.

### Bi-temporal modelling: what it represents, and what it structurally cannot

SQL:2011 standardises **two independent, orthogonal period mechanisms**, not one "bitemporal" feature †[220]. Application time is user-controlled (`PERIOD FOR p (start,end)`, closed-open, `UPDATE/DELETE ... FOR PORTION OF` which auto-splits overlapping rows, `PRIMARY KEY (k, p WITHOUT OVERLAPS)`, period-qualified foreign keys). System time is system-maintained (`GENERATED ALWAYS AS ROW START/END`, `WITH SYSTEM VERSIONING`, queried via `FOR SYSTEM_TIME AS OF`). A "bitemporal table" is simply both on one table; the standard never defines the term. **Critically: SQL:2011 adds no period data type** — a period is metadata over two ordinary, named, user-visible columns. The vocabulary (valid time vs transaction time) predates it †[221].

The standard enforces a deliberate **asymmetry**: on system-versioned tables users may not set or modify the system columns, may not update or delete historical rows, and constraints are enforced only on current rows; on application-time tables the user supplies and may rewrite the period, and every constraint must be interval-aware †[220]. Two integrity regimes: "what we recorded, when" is append-only and cheap; "what was true, when" is editable and expensive — and is the part you build yourself if the engine lacks it.

| Engine | Transaction time | Valid time | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL 18 (2025) | no | constraints only | `WITHOUT OVERLAPS` and `PERIOD` FKs; **no** application-time period tables, no `FOR PORTION OF`, no system versioning †[222] |
| MariaDB | yes | yes | but forbids `DELETE/UPDATE FOR PORTION` on a table that also has system time †[223] |
| Datomic | yes | **no** | `(E,A,V,Tx,op)` + `:db/txInstant`; "change is represented by accumulating the new, not by modifying or removing the old" †[224] |
| XTDB | yes | yes | bitemporal by default, four auto-maintained temporal columns, "inspired by SQL:2011" †[225] |

**The decisive negative, and the pivot of this whole section: neither bi-temporal databases nor slowly changing dimensions can represent live disagreement at all.** `WITHOUT OVERLAPS` and the single-current-row rule together guarantee **exactly one row per key at any (application-time, system-time) coordinate**. A "correction" is a later transaction-time assertion superseding an earlier one — that is *latest-wins with an audit trail*. Two contemporaneous competing live claims are a **constraint violation**, not a state †[220][226]. Kimball's SCD taxonomy is a set of eight lossy choices about change over time; Types 6 and 7 are the closest analogue (dual keys / embedded current-and-historical attributes so a fact can be grouped by either the as-was or the as-is value) and are the right *shape* to copy for dual addressability — but they model change at different times, never disagreement at one time †[227][228].

So: **live disagreement is not a temporal feature.** It requires a third dimension — the claimant — that the temporal standard does not provide. Budget it as a separate axis with its own keys.

One inherited design lesson is worth taking literally. TSQL2's standardisation attempt failed and SQL/Temporal Part 7 was cancelled in 2001, because hidden/implicit period columns are incompatible with SQL's rule that all row information be captured as column values, and because "statement modifiers" that silently change query semantics "contained no clear rules specifying the semantics" †[220]; Darwen & Date reach the same conclusion from relational theory — a table with hidden attributes "is not a relation" and "clearly violates The Information Principle" †[226]. **Do not build a temporal mode that rewrites ordinary queries, and do not hide the temporal columns.** Every validity and status attribute should be nameable, selectable and joinable like any other field.

### Belief revision, TMS, paraconsistency: what to borrow and what to leave

| Tradition | Verdict for kc | Grade |
|---|---|---|
| **AGM revision** [229][230] | **Wrong architecture, by construction.** The Success postulate forces the new input to be believed and Consistency forces a consistent result — AGM is *definitionally* a winner-picking operator. It also assumes logically closed (infinite) belief sets that "resource-bounded agents cannot implement", covers single-shot revision only, and its Recovery postulate is contested | Strong (theory) † |
| Katsuno–Mendelzon **revision vs update** [231] | Borrow this one distinction: new information about a static world vs a world that changed maps exactly onto transaction time vs valid time. It is the cleanest justification for two time axes | Strong (theory) † |
| **Non-prioritized** revision: semi-revision, screened/shielded operators, belief *bases*, symmetric merging where "neither dominates" [230][232] | Borrow: an incoming claim is **not** automatically believed, merging treats sources symmetrically, and you store asserted claims — never their deductive closure | Moderate † |
| **ATMS** [233] (vs Doyle's JTMS [234]) | Closest existing engineering pattern for *keeping* contradictions: label each datum with the assumption-sets under which it holds, so mutually inconsistent contexts coexist and are queried simultaneously | Moderate; **no modern systems evaluation found** † |
| **Provenance semirings** [235] | The formally-grounded modern version: an algebra for propagating "which source-set supports this" through relational query evaluation. If disagreement must survive joins and aggregation rather than existing only at leaf records, this is the mechanism with a proven algebra | Moderate (theory strong, deployment unmeasured) † |
| **Abstract argumentation** [236] | Attack relations + extension semantics decide what is collectively acceptable *without deleting the defeated* — the defeasible-reasoning analogue of the same idea | Moderate (theory) † |
| **Paraconsistent logic** [237][238] | Adopt the four-state **vocabulary** (asserted / denied / contested / unknown) — cheap, and it names the states you need. Do **not** adopt the inference machinery: every located application is philosophical, expert-system or theoretical, with **no** benchmark or controlled comparison showing a paraconsistent store beating a resolved one downstream | **Weak** — vocabulary only † |

The one genuinely actionable formal result: querying an inconsistent store for what all consistent readings agree on is **not** prohibitively expensive for the query shapes a knowledge layer issues. For self-join-free Boolean conjunctive queries under primary keys, `CERTAINTY(q)` is either in symmetric stratified Datalog (hence LOGSPACE, frequently first-order rewritable) or coNP-complete — **nothing in between** — and "queries involving foreign-to-primary key matches, the most typical join condition, fall on the logspace side" †[239][240]. Path queries with self-joins give a tetrachotomy, and which case applies is decidable in polynomial time in query size †[241]. The framework is Arenas, Bertossi & Chomicki's: a repair is a maximal consistent subinstance, a consistent answer holds in every repair †[242]. Concretely: a `certain` query mode can sit alongside a `best-rank` mode, with queries classified **offline at schema-design time** and coNP-side queries rejected or degraded rather than run.

### Is there a tested representation that keeps live disagreement first-class?

**Representation: yes, one, deployed at scale.** Wikidata's statement model is the only field-tested design in this space †[243][244]. An item carries many statements per property; each statement carries qualifiers (point in time, start/end time, determination method), **its own references**, and a **rank** from three values — *preferred* ("the most current statement or statements that best represent consensus"), *normal* (the default, carrying "no judgement or evaluation of a value's accuracy and currency"), and *deprecated* (statements "known to include errors ... or that represent outdated knowledge"). Query semantics is **best rank**: preferred if any exist, else normal; deprecated excluded by default but retrievable. Deprecated statements are **retained, not deleted**, specifically so the error is not re-added by someone else and so the evolution of knowledge is documented — and all statements, deprecated included, must remain verifiable.

That is the reference architecture, and it answers the expiry question directly: **retain + mark + exclude by default + filter before ranking**, not delete.

The plumbing for the surrounding vocabulary is standardised; the *status* vocabulary is not. PROV-O has been a W3C Recommendation since 2013 and gives you `prov:Entity` / `prov:Activity` / `prov:Agent` plus `wasGeneratedBy` / `wasDerivedFrom` / `wasAttributedTo`, explicitly designed to be extended — do not invent a provenance vocabulary [245]. RDF 1.2 (CR snapshot, April 2026) adds triple terms reachable via `rdf:reifies`, where "a reifier may denote ... a statement or belief that the proposition holds" — i.e. you can say *Bob claims X* **without asserting X**, which is precisely the primitive live disagreement needs †[246]; named graphs were the prior mechanism †[247] and nanopublications package assertion + provenance + publication-info as one citable unit †[248]. Temporal RDF (TKDE 2007) established labelling triples with valid-time intervals and the associated entailment and query semantics, and is the right prior art for the time half of the vocabulary †[249]. What none of these standardise is the conflict relation itself (contradicts / supersedes / restates / scopes-differently) or the status/rank vocabulary — those are where custom terms are justified, and Wikidata's three ranks are the only field-tested option. Note they have never been ablated against a finer or coarser vocabulary: "three ranks" is convention, not an evidenced optimum.

**Evidence that it beats latest-wins downstream: no. Not at the store level.** This is the section's central honest finding, and it must not be softened. The available evidence splits into three piles, none of which is that result:

1. **Annotation-level (strong, but a learning-target result, not a retrieval-store result).** Modelling each annotator as a separate task gives performance "equivalent or superior" to aggregated-label training across seven tasks, with uncertainty estimates that "correlate better with annotation disagreements" †[250]; +22% on predicting individual ratings and +33% on predicting annotator variance †[251]; soft-label training "matched or exceeded hard-label accuracy on every dataset", cut KL divergence to the annotator distribution by 32%, and produced per-sample entropy correlating 61% more strongly with annotator entropy †[252]; CrowdTruth's disagreement-preserving aggregation beat majority vote on four heterogeneous tasks †[253]; a crowd-distribution signal drives abstention competitively with selective-prediction baselines †[254]; and there is a three-edition shared task with public data and metrics †[255]. **The win here is in calibration and abstention, not headline accuracy — do not promise accuracy gains.**
2. **Negative (moderate–strong).** Unmanaged conflicting and outdated content in a retrieval pool measurably *hurts* downstream accuracy even when the correct content is present †[194][195], which argues against an undifferentiated keep-everything pool but not for any particular structured alternative.
3. **One near-direct test (weak).** NuggetIndex stores atomic units as managed records, each with "links to evidence, a temporal validity interval, and a lifecycle state", filtering invalid or deprecated records **before** ranking; it reports +42% nugget recall, +9pp temporal correctness "without the recall collapse observed in time-filtered baselines", a 55% reduction in conflict rate and 64% shorter generator input †[256]. Single preprint, self-reported numbers, partly self-constructed datasets, no independent replication. Its most useful contribution is the negative one: **naive date-cutoff filtering causes recall collapse**, so the lifecycle state must be richer than a date.

And latest-wins is a **hard baseline**, not a straw man. In temporal knowledge graphs a training-free baseline that simply predicts recurring facts "requires little hyperparameter tuning and no iterative training" and ranks first or third against 11 published methods on three of five standard datasets †[257]; TGB 2.0 (8 datasets, up to 53M edges) finds "simple heuristic approaches often rival complex methods" and that scalability, not modelling, binds †[258]. Implement "most recently asserted" and "most recurrent" as first-class baselines and make them the bar before building anything.

### The design answer

**Model conflict explicitly in the representation; collapse it in the default view; build no inference machinery.** Each half of that is separately supported.

| Decision | Call | Why |
|---|---|---|
| N claims per (entity, property), each with its own source, time qualifiers and status | **Do it** | The only deployed representation of live disagreement works this way †[243]; bitemporal tables structurally cannot hold it †[220]; it is cheap and reversible |
| Default query view resolves to one answer (best-rank / latest-wins), raw view does not | **Do it** | Latest-wins is a hard baseline †[257][258]; Wikidata's best-rank semantics is the field-tested shape †[243] |
| Validity interval + lifecycle state per **assertion**, filtered **before** ranking | **Do it** | Per-fact duration beats uniform TTL [197]; relevance prefers stale [191][192]; coexisting outdated content degrades output even when current content is present †[194] |
| Retain deprecated content, exclude by default, never delete | **Do it** | Wikidata's documented rationale †[243]; naive date cutoffs collapse recall †[256] |
| Numeric per-source trust field consumed by ranker/decoder | **Do it** | Weighting and training work; prompt annotation does not [203][204][206] |
| Deduplicate; count distinct sources; cap per source and per side | **Do it** | Repetition reverses credibility preference [206]; N documents per side is an implicit vote-by-volume |
| Reuse PROV-O field names; define only the conflict/status terms | **Do it** | Stable 13-year standard, designed for extension [245] |
| Four-state status vocabulary (asserted / denied / contested / unknown) | **Do it** | Cheap; names the states — but adopt the vocabulary only †[238] |
| Paraconsistent inference, AGM revision operators, learned truth-discovery scorer in v1 | **Do not** | No evaluation showing any of them pays off †[238][230][209][210] |
| Semiring/ATMS annotation propagating support-sets through joins | **Defer behind a seam** | Right mechanism if disagreement must survive aggregation, but no modern systems evaluation †[235][233] |
| Measure the **base rate of conflict on your own corpus** before freezing the schema | **Do this first** | Highest-value action in the review: if genuine inter-context conflict is 0.1% of queries this is over-engineering; if 10%, load-bearing. Cheap: NLI over top-k on a query sample |

**What to measure.** Do not report raw RAG accuracy. Stratify by *(sufficient context, conflicting context, neither)* and report selective accuracy and coverage, otherwise a retrieval improvement and an abstention improvement are indistinguishable in the metric [208]. Add "rate at which a correct prior is overridden by a wrong retrieved claim" — accuracy alone hides it [181]. Adopt the TREC Health Misinformation judgement shape: three orthogonal judgements (usefulness, correctness, credibility) mapped to a graded ordering in which the *worst* class is useful + incorrect + **credible**, with separate helpful and harmful qrels, and compatibility rather than nDCG as the primary measure because "it is tempting to use the above scores to compute nDCG, but that ignores the incorrect information" †[259]. Its second task — total recall of misinformation in the collection — is the right internal QA measure for *how much of the disagreement your store is even aware of*. For a dashboard number ("how much disagreement is in the store"), prefer a cheap structural measure — count of entities with more than one non-deprecated competing claim, max spread per property — over a logic-based inconsistency measure, whose complexity is itself the subject of a research literature †[260] (weakly sourced: bibliographic record only). Finally, treat every conflict threshold as a per-deployment calibration parameter: an audit of eight automatic attribution scorers found per-dataset rankings **invert**, with one NLI scorer dropping from 0.90 AUROC on short-form text to 0.53 — chance — on long-form [261].

### What the evidence does not support

**"Bitemporal modelling lets you represent disagreement — you keep both versions, so both viewpoints survive."** False as stated, and structurally so. SQL:2011's `WITHOUT OVERLAPS` on application-time primary keys and the single-current-row rule on system-versioned tables both guarantee exactly one row per key at any (application-time, system-time) coordinate; a later transaction-time row *supersedes* the earlier one. That is latest-wins with an audit trail. Two live, contemporaneous, competing assertions are a **constraint violation** in the model, not a state it can hold †[220][226]. Slowly changing dimensions have the same limit: they model change over time, never disagreement at a time †[227].

**"SQL:2011 gave the industry a portable temporal database, so temporal modelling is solved."** Substantially overstated. The standard's own authors list period joins, period aggregates, period set operations, period coalescing, multiple application-time periods per table and non-temporal periods as *future* "Language Opportunities", and there is no period data type at all †[220]. Fourteen years on, adoption is thin: PostgreSQL 18 shipped only the constraints, MariaDB forbids `FOR PORTION OF` on bitemporal tables, Datomic has no valid time †[222][223][224].

**"TSQL2 / the Snodgrass approach is the canonical temporal model the standard adopted."** The opposite. The TSQL2-based ISO submission generated "considerable controversy", failed to gain support, and SQL/Temporal Part 7 was cancelled outright in 2001; SQL:2011 deliberately took a different approach because hidden/implicit period columns are incompatible with SQL's requirement that all row information be captured as column values †[220][226].

**"AGM belief revision is the theory you want for conflicting knowledge."** AGM is a theory of *eliminating* conflict by construction — Success forces the input to be believed, Consistency forces a consistent outcome — and is unimplementable as specified (infinite deductively-closed belief sets), single-shot only, with a contested Recovery postulate †[229][230]. Only the non-prioritized fringe is relevant.

**"Paraconsistent logic is the proven way to build a contradiction-tolerant database."** Unsupported by any located evaluation. The formal systems are mature and Belnap's motivation is explicit and old, but every cited application is philosophical, theoretical, or expert-system anecdote; **no benchmark, field study or controlled comparison** was found in which a paraconsistent store outperformed a repaired or resolved one on a downstream task †[237][238].

**"Truth discovery / source-reliability weighting solves multi-source conflict."** Not supported.
