RFC 5155 introduced NSEC3 specifically to resist zone enumeration; RFC 9276 (BCP 236) then recommends **one iteration and an empty salt**, on the grounds that an adversary willing to spend CPU "will likely be able to find most of the 'guessable' names despite any level of additional hashing iterations," that additional iterations show "increasing performance costs yet diminishing returns," and that a constant salt "does not change the cost of the attack" [355][356]. If existence must be hidden, hide it structurally — separate index, separate tenant — not by output suppression or identifier obfuscation.

### Can a ReBAC system pre-filter a candidate set?

This is the question that decides whether Zanzibar-family authorization can sit in a retrieval path at all, and the vendors answer it in their own documentation. The answer is: **per-document checks are cheap; "which of these N" is cheap only up to about 10³–10⁴ objects, or via a materialised view.**

| Primitive | Shape | Documented limit | Verdict for retrieval |
|---|---|---|---|
| SpiceDB `LookupResources` | Pre-filter: return the resource IDs a subject may see | Usable "if the number of resources that a user has access to is small (e.g. less than 10,000)"; "can get slow quickly" beyond | Fine for small corpora and per-collection scoping; not a general pre-filter [349] |
| SpiceDB `CheckBulkPermissions` | Post-filter: batch-check a page of candidates | "We don't know how many results are going to be accessible beforehand," so you loop until a page fills; "works better with… cursor-based pagination than limit-offset" | Loses total counts and offset pagination; safe only if scoring was already view-restricted [349] |
| SpiceDB `Materialize` | "Authzed's version of the Leopard cache referenced in the Zanzibar paper" — a local copy of who may see what | Requires operating a denormalised store fed by a change stream | The scalable answer; "ACL-aware filtering again becomes a simple JOIN" [349] |
| OpenFGA `ListObjects` | Pre-filter | Returns "the results found within the time allotted (`listObjectsDeadline`, default 3s) up to… `listObjectsMaxResults` (default 1000)" | **Silently incomplete and non-deterministic.** Used as a search pre-filter at scale it drops documents the user *is* entitled to see — a recall bug that presents as a ranking regression [357] |
| Zanzibar `Expand` + offline index | Denormalisation | Google states Expand "allows [clients] to build efficient search indices for access-controlled content"; Drive-style search "often issue[s] tens to hundreds of authorization checks to serve a single set of search results," which forced request hedging | Google's own answer to access-controlled search is denormalisation, not per-hit checks [348] |

Shipped vendor practice is a **hybrid**, not pure early or pure late binding: bind stable principal *identifiers* (users, groups, deny entries) into the index at ingest, resolve the caller's identity → principal set at query time, and apply that set as a filter inside the query. Microsoft 365 Copilot connectors attach a grant/deny ACL array per `externalItem`, deny taking precedence, and warn explicitly: "Avoid expanding the membership of your external groups directly into the ACLs of individual items because each group membership can lead to a high volume of item updates" [345]. Google Cloud Search requires items to be indexed with their repository ACLs and forces an explicit inheritance type per item — `BOTH_PERMIT`, `CHILD_OVERRIDE`, `PARENT_OVERRIDE` — evaluated leaf-to-root [346]. Azure AI Search documents a filterable, non-retrievable `Collection(Edm.String)` of principal IDs plus `search.in()`, and warns that expressing the same predicate as a disjunction of equalities "slows down query response time by many seconds" at hundreds-to-thousands of values versus subsecond for `search.in` [347]. Two rules follow: never denormalise *group membership* into per-document ACLs, and represent the filter as a set-membership primitive rather than a boolean disjunction.

Conflict resolution should be a named combining algorithm, not ad-hoc precedence. XACML 3.0 normatively defines `deny-overrides`, `ordered-deny-overrides`, `permit-overrides`, `deny-unless-permit`, `permit-unless-deny`, `first-applicable` and `only-one-applicable`; §9.1.6 states that `NotApplicable` should map to Deny, and that treating it as Permit is safe only in a closed environment where every requester uses exactly the expected syntax; §9.1.7 warns that negative rules "can lead to policy violations, therefore some authorities recommend that they not be used," naming attribute suppression and base-group redefinition as the failure cases and recommending a distinct group over a per-individual deny [358]. NIST SP 800-162 is the companion vocabulary for the attribute-based half [359].

One open question is whether relationship tuples are expressively sufficient at all for **content-derived** permissions — "documents mentioning project X are restricted", "anything classified above Confidential". That is a predicate shape, not a relationship shape; XACML handles it natively and Zanzibar-family systems bolt it on through caveats/conditions, but the two families have not been compared empirically on search workloads. The choice matters downstream because it determines whether the retrieval-time filter is a set-membership test or an arbitrary predicate — which in turn determines the filtered-ANN strategy.

### Multi-tenant isolation and filtered ANN

For vector retrieval the ACL predicate is a **recall** problem before it is a latency problem. Graph indexes suffer "connectivity breakdown on low-selectivity filtered queries, where qualifying vectors become sparse and the graph structure among them fragments" — precisely the enterprise regime where each principal sees a small slice [360]. How the predicate is encoded dominates throughput by up to three orders of magnitude: Curator reduces low-selectivity latency by up to 20.9x versus a pre-filtering fallback at +5.5% build time and +4.3% memory [360][361]; HONEYBEE reports 13.5x lower latency than row-level security at 1.24x memory, matching dedicated per-role indexes with 90.4% less extra memory [362]; ACORN reports 2–1,000x higher throughput at fixed recall [363]; and the NeurIPS'23 BigANN filtered track shows a ~12x QPS spread (2,291 to 37,671) at recall@10 ≥ 0.9 on identical data and hardware, which the organisers attribute "mainly [to] a better handling of the filtering constraints, with more appropriate data structures" [364].

The design space is bracketed cleanly, though the framing rests partly on preprint and workshop material **[unverified review]**: a single global index "wastes search effort on unauthorized vectors and degrades recall," while an oracle index built per authorized set "duplicates every shared vector between roles or queries" [365]; Yalamarthi & Pappachan name the resulting three-way tension between policy correctness, ANN recall and latency, and note fine-grained access control "is not fully supported in modern vector databases" [366]. Every published middle ground exploits the *structure of the permission lattice* — HONEYBEE partitions on the RBAC role "thin waist", Curator nests per-tenant clustering trees inside a shared tree, Veda builds an access-aware lattice with copy/merge under a storage budget. The practical implication is a schema decision taken **before ingest**: model permissions as a lattice of role/group combinations and partition on it, not as an arbitrary per-document predicate over one global index.

Measurement follows the same rule. Recall@k must be computed against the **authorized** ground truth, not the global one; the BigANN filtered workload (set-containment over a 200,386-tag vocabulary, 1–2 tags per query) is structurally the same shape as an ACL predicate and is a usable proxy benchmark [364].

### Embeddings and RAG as leakage surfaces

Storing embeddings is not a privacy boundary. Song & Raghunathan recover 50–70% of input words as a bag of words from popular sentence embeddings [367]; Morris et al.'s Vec2Text frames inversion as controlled generation with iterative re-embedding and recovers 92% of 32-token inputs *exactly* (BLEU 97.3) against a black-box encoder, plus 89% of full names from embedded MIMIC-III clinical notes [368]. Later work removes the two limiting assumptions: Huang et al. attack via a surrogate without access to the victim encoder [369], and ZSInvert is zero-shot and query-efficient, working "for any text embedding without training an embedding-specific inversion model" [370] (preprint **[unverified review]**). The operative conclusion is architectural: **a vector store crossing a trust boundary should be treated as holding the text at chunk granularity**, so it must sit inside the same permission boundary as the documents.

Two cheap, retrieval-neutral defences are documented, graded *moderate* **[unverified review]**: dimension reduction and product quantization "prove to be effective at significantly reducing reconstructibility, while maintaining retrieval effectiveness," and a per-user secret constant transformation degrades reconstruction almost completely with mathematically identical retrieval effectiveness [371]; the independent RecSys 2025 reproducibility study concurs that quantization mitigates the risk and is "a simpler and more widely applicable solution" than additive Gaussian noise [372]. Caveat carried from the source review: the secret transformation is reversible by anyone who learns the constant, and both papers evaluate against inverters trained on *untransformed* embeddings — defence in depth, not a boundary. The same work reports that mean pooling and bottleneck pre-training *increase* reconstructibility, making pooling strategy an inversion-risk parameter, not only a quality one.

RAG relocates leakage from weights to datastore and makes it verbatim. Zeng et al. confirm RAG reduces leakage of the LLM's own training data, but with 250 untargeted prompts against an Enron datastore, 116 produced verbatim matches from retrieved content and 208 close paraphrases; targeted attacks extracted 89–256 PIIs per 250 prompts [373]. Huang et al. find kNN-LMs "more susceptible to leaking private information from their private datastore than parametric models" [374]. Qi et al. demonstrate the same extraction against *deployed* commercial products (OpenAI custom GPTs) and find chunking is a security parameter — reconstruction increases with fewer, longer, semantically clean chunks [375] (preprint **[unverified review]**). Bigger chunks retrieve better and leak more; that trade must be made deliberately.

Membership in the corpus is close to unhideable from anyone who can query. MEntA reports up to 0.991 AUC using five non-templated natural-language questions, with no shadow models and no surrogate, and "remains effective under state-of-the-art RAG defenses, while current detectors either miss MEntA or flag benign queries at high rates" [376]; earlier work established the attack in black-box/gray-box [377] and stealthy [378] settings. If the *existence* of a document is itself sensitive — an unannounced acquisition, an HR case — that content needs its own corpus partition and its own retriever, not a filter on a shared one.

Finally, enforcement layer. Indirect prompt injection through retrieved content compromises real LLM-integrated applications, which makes the retrieval corpus an attack channel into the permission logic itself [379]. Bhatt et al. argue that prompt sanitization, output filtering, system isolation and training-level privacy are "fundamentally probabilistic," and introduce the **participant-aware** rule: content must be authorized for *all* users involved in an interaction, not just the requester [380]; SD-RAG independently moves sanitization and disclosure control into the retrieval phase, reporting up to 58% privacy-score improvement plus injection resilience [381]. Both are preprints and neither framework is independently validated **[unverified review]**, and the source review found no implementation or evaluation of participant-aware enforcement at scale — but the position is consistent with the attack evidence, and the intersection rule is not the default anywhere, so it is easy to get wrong in any shared-session or multi-principal agent design.

### What standard practice actually is

A design can be held to the following. Items 1–6 rest on strong evidence; 7–9 are the *de facto* practice of the systems literature rather than an established standard.

1. **Bind stable principal identifiers into the index at ingest; resolve group membership outside the index at query time.** This hybrid is what Microsoft Graph connectors, Google Cloud Search and Azure AI Search all ship, and all three warn against expanding group membership into per-item ACLs [345][346][347].
2. **Apply the visibility predicate inside the retrieval operator, before scoring** — never as a post-ranking trim, never as a system-prompt instruction, never as an output classifier [342][379][380].
3. **Represent the filter as a set-membership primitive** (`search.in`-style), not a boolean disjunction of equalities [347].
4. **Pick a named combining algorithm from the XACML vocabulary, default `NotApplicable` to Deny, and prefer distinct groups over per-individual deny rules** [358].
5. **Make freshness a per-request parameter bound to the content version** (zookie-style), not a global staleness setting on the index [348].
6. **Do not promise exact total-hit counts in a permission-filtered UI**, and do not use limit-offset pagination over a post-filtered result set [344][349].
7. **Above ~10³–10⁴ accessible objects per principal, denormalise into a materialised permission index fed by a change stream** (Leopard / Materialize). Below that, a resource-ID pre-filter is fine — but never treat OpenFGA `ListObjects` as complete [348][349][357].
8. **Group content into permission-homogeneous collections** so the common case is one collection-level check, and co-locate permission data with the index; a remote PDP round trip per hit is what makes query-time authorization untenable [344].
9. **Partition the vector index on the permission lattice before ingest**, and measure recall@k against the authorized ground truth [362][360][365].

There is no accepted benchmark to hold a system to on the *quality* side. The source review searched arXiv, Crossref and OpenAlex and found no dataset pairing a corpus with realistic per-principal ACLs *and* relevance judgements; the RAG-security surveys catalogue attacks and defences and list none either [382]. Absence of evidence rather than proof, but strong enough to plan around: any serious design must build its own evaluation, measuring (a) recall@k against the authorized subset — the retrieval-quality cost of the filter — and (b) **leakage probes**: does the ranked list, the score, the count, or the latency change when a document the user cannot see is added or removed? (b) is the operationalisation of the Büttcher–Clarke channel, and nobody ships it.

### What the evidence does not support

**"Post-filtering after ranking is safe as long as you remove what the user can't see."** Directly contradicted, with both a constructive attack and a vendor admission. BM25 scores yield the exact global document frequency of any term in two queries; bare rank order yields it to ~0.5% relative error in about ten queries; phrase queries reconstruct the full content of an unreadable file [342]. Elastic's own documentation states DLS "doesn't affect global index statistics that relevancy scoring uses" and that a restricted user "could still learn about field names and terms that only exist in inaccessible documents, and count how many inaccessible documents contain a given term" [343]. The channel is the corpus statistics, not the document bodies.

**"Query-time (late) enforcement is the secure modern choice; index-time (early) binding is a legacy speed compromise."** Not supported as stated. The usual late-binding implementation — rank globally, then trim — is the *insecure* one; only query integration, which restricts the statistics too, is safe [342]. And late binding is most expensive exactly in the enterprise case: Bailey et al. find query times unacceptable for users who can see a small proportion of documents and when exact counts are required, and that off-server access checks are "dramatically" more expensive "even on a local network" [344]. Google's own paper says clients build denormalised search indices from `Expand`, and that Drive-style search issues "tens to hundreds of authorization checks to serve a single set of search results" [348]. Leopard, SpiceDB Materialize and the Microsoft/Google/Azure connector designs are all early binding of identifiers [345][346][347][349].

**"Early binding is unsafe because indexed ACLs go stale and revoked users keep seeing content."** Overstated. Staleness is a consistency problem with a published solution: Zanzibar's zookie binds each content version to a timestamp and forces checks at a snapshot at least that fresh, which is what prevents the "new enemy" problem — and late binding has the *same* failure if the check snapshot is not bounded by the content version [348]. Freshness is a per-request parameter, not a property of where you bind. What remains genuinely unresolved is *how* stale a materialised permission index may safely be: Zanzibar gives the mechanism but nobody has published a principled bound, a risk model, or an empirical distribution of time-from-revocation-to-harm.

**"Suppressing counts, or refusing to answer when the result set is small, prevents inference."** Contradicted since 1979. Denning, Denning & Schwartz: general trackers "always exist if there are enough distinguishable classes of individuals," "are almost always easy to find," permit "calculating statistics for arbitrary query sets, without requiring preknowledge of anything in the database," and — explicitly — "Security is not guaranteed by the lack of a general tracker" [350]. Adam & Worthmann conclude no single control prevents both exact and partial disclosure, and that perturbation methods introduce bias and still suffer the 0/1 query-set-size problem, which is the formal name for the "no results" leak [351].

**"Hashing identifiers (doc IDs, tenant IDs, ACL principals) hides which items exist in a shared index."** Contradicted by IETF operational experience with precisely this design. RFC 9276 (BCP 236) states that determined adversaries "will likely be able to find most of the 'guessable' names despite any level of additional hashing iterations," that names leak through email logs, certificate transparency, web links and malware scanners, that additional iterations show "increasing performance costs yet diminishing returns," and that a constant salt "does not change the cost of the attack" and is "functionally useless" — recommending one iteration and an empty salt [355][356]. The standards body stopped pretending the hashing was doing much work.

**"A ReBAC service can just hand you the list of documents a user can see."** Only within limits the vendors document themselves. SpiceDB: `LookupResources` is usable "if the number of resources that a user has access to is small (e.g. less than 10,000)" and "can get slow quickly" beyond [349]. OpenFGA: `ListObjects` returns "the results found within the time allotted (`listObjectsDeadline`, default: 3s) up to the maximum number of results configured (`listObjectsMaxResults`, default: 1000)" — a silently incomplete, non-deterministic answer [357]. Used as a search pre-filter at scale this drops documents the user is legitimately entitled to see, and it presents as a mysterious ranking regression rather than as an error.

**"Post-filtering is merely slower; correctness is unaffected."** False on two counts. Graph ANN indexes hit connectivity breakdown on low-selectivity filtered queries — the qualifying set fragments and recall drops, which is exactly the enterprise regime [360][362][366]. And SpiceDB's own guidance notes that with post-filtering "we don't know how many results are going to be accessible beforehand," so you must over-fetch in a loop, you cannot serve limit-offset pagination correctly, and you cannot report a total count [349].

**"Storing embeddings instead of text is privacy-preserving."** Contradicted: 50–70% of input words recoverable as bag-of-words [367]; 92% of 32-token inputs recovered exactly and 89% of full names from clinical notes [368]; the attack no longer needs the victim encoder [369] nor a per-embedding trained inverter [370]. This is the belief the whole embedding-inversion literature exists to kill.

**"…and therefore a vector store is exactly as sensitive as plaintext, at any chunk size."** Also *not* supported — the headline number is narrower than it is quoted. The 92%-exact figure is for 32-token, in-domain inputs with a model trained for that specific encoder and length. The RecSys 2025 reproducibility study replicates in-domain results but finds inversion models "sensitive to the length of the input text" with "degraded performance when applied to out-of-domain sequence lengths" (on Signal1M, reproduced Vec2Text BLEU 57.7 versus 80.7 reported), and notes the 32-token models behind the headline were partly unreleased [372]; Zhuang et al. report correcting "an embedding model implementation error and a testing data leakage error in the original Vec2Text experimental setting" [371]. **No one has demonstrated reconstruction of a 512-token production RAG chunk from an undisclosed encoder**, and no reconstruction-quality-versus-chunk-length curve has been published — which is the single number a designer needs. Quantization and dimension reduction further cut reconstructibility at no retrieval cost, so the risk is a design parameter rather than a constant.

**"RAG is the privacy-safe alternative to fine-tuning — the data stays in the datastore, not the weights."** Half true and misleading. Zeng et al. confirm the first half but show the datastore is highly extractable: 116 of 250 untargeted prompts produced verbatim matches from retrieved Enron content [373]. Huang et al. find kNN-LMs more susceptible to leaking datastore contents than parametric models [374]. Qi et al. extract datastore contents from shipped commercial products [375]. RAG relocates the leak from an aggregated, noisy channel (weights) to a precise, verbatim one (retrieved context), and makes it reachable by anyone who can send a prompt.

**"Instructing the model not to reveal restricted content, or filtering the output, is an adequate control."** Contradicted from several directions. Indirect prompt injection through retrieved content overrides system instructions in real deployed applications [379]. MEntA "remains effective under state-of-the-art RAG defenses, while current detectors either miss MEntA or flag benign queries at high rates" [376]. Anderson et al.'s own template-instruction defence "shows high effectiveness for *some* datasets and models" — which is not a control [377]. Bhatt et al.'s position is that all such defences are "fundamentally probabilistic" [380].

**"Encrypted/searchable search is thoroughly broken by leakage-abuse attacks, so there's no point considering it."** Overstated in the other direction. Blackstone, Kamara & Moataz "highlight the practical limitations and assumptions underlying the often-cited IKK and Count attacks" [383][384] and conclude after systematic experiments that "the only setting that resulted in reasonable recovery rates under practical assumptions was the case of high-selectivity queries with a leakage profile that includes the response identity pattern… and the volume pattern. All other attack scenarios either failed or relied on unrealistic assumptions (e.g., very high known-data rates)" [385]. The LEAKER SoK, evaluating the major attacks on real datasets with real user queries, found "some attacks work better on real-world data than previously thought and that others perform worse" [386]. The honest reading: the response-identity + volume channel is real; most headline query-recovery results are not reachable in practice.

**"Published membership-inference AUCs against RAG can be taken at face value as real-world risk."** Treat with caution — the adjacent literature has a documented evaluation confound nobody has checked for in the RAG setting. Duan et al. find MIAs on LLM pretraining data perform "barely better than random guessing" once the member/non-member split is not distribution-shifted [387], and Das, Zhang & Tramèr show blind baselines using surface text alone beat published MIAs on foundation models because "evaluations of MI attacks for foundation models are flawed" in how members and non-members are sampled [388]. RAG MIA is arguably less exposed (the attacker often holds the candidate document verbatim, and the corpus is a defined set), but the source review found **no paper applying the blind-baseline critique to RAG MIA**, so the 0.991 AUC figure has not been stress-tested the way the LLM numbers were. Flagged in the source as the reviewer's own inference rather than a published finding, and the source review had no adversarial verification pass — so this is a caution, not a refutation.

**Provenance failures — claims traceable to no primary source.** Three assertions widely repeated in vendor and practitioner material could not be grounded in any primary source by the source review: (i) that there exists a public benchmark or agreed metric for *permission-aware retrieval quality* — searches of arXiv, Crossref and OpenAlex found no dataset pairing a corpus with per-principal ACLs and relevance judgements, and the RAG-security surveys list none [382]; (ii) that the Büttcher–Clarke score/rank side channel is known *not* to apply to dense retrievers and shared ANN graphs — no study of the question was found in either direction; (iii) that participant-aware (intersection-of-entitlements) enforcement is workable at scale for multi-party agent sessions — proposed in a position paper [380], not implemented or evaluated anywhere located. In all three cases the correct statement is "unknown", not "fine".

## Agent memory, provenance and abstention

**Verification status of this section.** All three underlying topic reviews (agent memory, provenance standards, abstention/calibration) were compiled *without* an adversarial verification pass. Findings drawn from primary standards text, peer-reviewed benchmarks and specifications read in full are reported at face value; findings that rest on a single unreplicated preprint, on a vendor report, or on a reviewer's own negative search result are flagged inline as **[unverified pass]** and should be treated as leads to test rather than premises to build on. Evidence grades used throughout: **strong** (peer-reviewed or primary-standard, methodology public, ideally corroborated), **moderate** (single credible study, or credible-but-interested source with disclosed methodology), **weak** (single-author/unreplicated preprint, small n, or absence-of-evidence), **contested** (the literature actively disagrees).

### Agent memory is a research area with one clean result and a great deal of unrefereed noise

The single most design-relevant number in the memory literature is not a system comparison at all — it is an ablation *inside one model*. On LongMemEval-S (~115k-token histories), GPT-4o scores 0.870 when handed only the oracle evidence sessions and 0.606 when handed the full history: a 30.3% relative drop. Llama-3.1-70B drops 55.1% (0.744 → 0.334) and Phi-3-128k 45.9% [389]. The model is not compute-limited, it is distraction-limited. **Strong.** This is the number that justifies building a selection layer at all, and it does so with no vendor system in the loop; it also sets the ceiling any retrieval design is chasing, which is why an oracle-evidence upper bound belongs in the evaluation harness as a first-class figure. An independent replication on the same benchmark by Chroma reports ~80% on focused ~300-token prompts against ~55% on the full 113k-token history for Claude Sonnet 4, and ~75% vs ~50% for GPT-4.1, across 18 models from four vendors [390]. **Moderate** — the source is a vector-database company whose product benefits from the conclusion, though the methodology and tasks are fully disclosed.

Whether long context obviates retrieval turns entirely on whether the corpus is *bounded*, not on how large the window is. For corpora that fit, long context beats RAG on average [95], a 4K model with simple retrieval matches a 16K fine-tuned long-context model at far lower compute [391], and long-context models rival specialised retrieval pipelines on LOFT [392]. **Strong**, from three independent studies — and none of those settings is agent memory, where history is unbounded and grows monotonically. The engineering rule that follows is unusually crisp: do not build a memory system for a bounded knowledge base that fits the window; stuff the window.

What the long-context benchmarks actually show, as distinct from what their headline numbers are used to claim:

| Benchmark | Finding | What it kills | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|
| HELMET [393] | Most long-context models achieve near-perfect needle-in-a-haystack while showing large gaps on real tasks; HELMET's own task categories correlate poorly with each other | NIAH as a proxy for usable context; rank transfer across task types | strong |
| RULER [394] | Only about half of models claiming ≥32K windows maintain satisfactory performance at 32K | The advertised window as a budgetable capacity | strong |
| NoLiMa [395] | Remove lexical overlap between question and needle and 11 of 13 models claiming 128K+ fall below half their short-context baseline at 32K (GPT-4o 99.3% → 69.7%) | Any retrieval evaluation whose items share vocabulary with the stored text | strong |
| LongPiBench [396] | Across five commercial and six open-source models, "most current models are robust against the 'lost in the middle' issue"; the surviving bias is driven by *distance between* relevant pieces, not absolute position | The 2023 mitigation ("put important things at the edges") as a current design rule | moderate, **contested** |
| Liu et al. [169] | The original U-shaped positional degradation, real for its model generation | — | strong for 2023 models |

Lost-in-the-middle is the clearest case where a widely-repeated design rule has outlived its evidence, but it is genuinely **contested**: mechanistic work continues to model the U-shape as architecturally real, and the ACL 2025 Findings result is one benchmark. The safe reading is that the effect that survives is co-location — facts that must be reasoned over jointly should be adjacent in the assembled context — while absolute head/tail placement is no longer the lever it was. A second, awkward finding from the same Chroma report is that all 18 models performed *better* on shuffled, incoherent haystacks than on logically structured ones [390]; that is the opposite of the common instinct to reconstruct document order when assembling context, and it is a vendor result on an unverified pass, so it is a hypothesis to test rather than a rule to adopt.

### Benchmark validity: who built the benchmark, and did anyone else run it

The founding architecture of this area has never been refereed. MemGPT — the origin of the virtual-context-management framing, ~1,195 citations — remains an arXiv preprint (v2, Feb 2024) with no peer-reviewed venue [397]. Its evaluation introduced Deep Memory Retrieval, a benchmark defined by the same team, now effectively saturated: a competing vendor reports 94.8% against MemGPT's 93.4%, a 1.4-point margin [398]. **Strong** (primary sources: arXiv listing, venue record, the competitor's own paper). The idea — OS-style paging of context — is worth borrowing freely; the empirical claim has no independent refereeing behind it.

LOCOMO, the benchmark most memory systems report on, *is* peer-reviewed (ACL 2024) and reasonably constructed — machine-human pipeline, persona and temporal-event-graph grounded, human-verified [399]. Two properties nonetheless limit its use as a superiority test: its dialogues are LLM-generated and then LLM-judged by overlapping model families, and the published version's own description of the data differs from the preprint's (600 turns / 16K tokens / up to 32 sessions vs ~300 turns / 9K tokens / up to 35 sessions), so papers citing "LOCOMO's size" do not agree with one another. **Strong** (both primary sources read directly).

Reported LOCOMO numbers are not comparable across papers, and the spread is enormous. Independent evaluations place memory systems at 21–54% where system papers self-report 68–99% on the same benchmark; six unreported protocol variables — ingestion granularity, raw-text preservation, retrieval budget, retrieval modality, rubric audit, judge choice — move rankings, and in one documented case a convincing win vanished once retrieval budget was equalised (the winner had been consuming 2.6M characters of retrieved context per query) [400][401][402]. **Moderate** — two independent preprints plus a cost-focused third-party comparison, converging with the wide numeric spread visible across the ~25 LOCOMO-reporting preprints the source review surveyed. **[unverified pass]** on the survey's base-rate claim specifically.

| Source | Ordering on LOCOMO | Interest | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|
| Independent reproducible testbed [402] | mem0, plain RAG and full-context cluster at 77–81%; Graphiti (Zep) and cognee reach 55–56%; plain RAG matches the top cluster at **8.4× lower total cost of ownership** than mem0; gap attributed to retrieval *incompleteness*, not reasoning | none — measures accuracy, latency, CPU, RAM, disk, network for all arms | moderate |
| Mem0 vendor paper [403] | Mem0 ahead of a MemGPT baseline | vendor evaluating its own product with LLM-as-judge | weak as a comparison |
| Zep vendor paper [398] | Zep ahead of MemGPT on a MemGPT-defined benchmark | vendor | weak as a comparison |
| Letta engineering blog [404] | States it could not reproduce Mem0's MemGPT baseline (Mem0 did not respond to requests for clarification or supply the modified implementation) and measures 74.0% where Mem0 reported 68.5% | vendor, but the load-bearing content is a first-party statement that its own system's baseline was misreported | moderate for the reproduction failure only |

Two measurement artefacts are larger than any architecture in this literature. Strict token-F1 and LLM-as-judge differ by **27.5 points on identical model outputs**; and changing only *which memory form receives retrieval credit* (raw stored form, source dialogue turn, or canonical representation) changes nDCG on 83.4–94.0% of shared queries without rerunning retrieval at all [405][406]. **Moderate** — two single-author rescoring audits, checkable in method but unreplicated **[unverified pass]**. Meanwhile the judge that nearly every memory claim rests on has documented position, verbosity and self-enhancement biases, and its famous ~80% human-agreement figure was measured on *chatbot response preference*, not factual memory recall [121]. **Strong** for the biases; the absence of any judge-human agreement study on an agent-memory benchmark is the source review's own negative search result — **weak, and explicitly an absence of evidence**.

The practical consequence is a reading prior rather than a ranking: assume a published win is a protocol artefact until retrieval budget, judge and baseline configuration are disclosed *and* equalised, and treat an internal A/B against a plain-RAG baseline as worth more than any published comparison.

### What the memory evidence supports about design

Cheap, measured, and inside a peer-reviewed benchmark's own ablations: index at **round** rather than session granularity, and store a **fact-augmented key** — extracted user facts concatenated with the verbatim value at index time — for an average +9.4% recall@k and +5.4% final accuracy across models [389]. **Moderate**, and the only concrete v1 retrieval recipe in this area measured against a proper baseline.

Structure buys retrieval cost, not answer quality. A systematic study of filesystem-organised agent memory found organised stores roughly halve retrieval cost on large material while "no agent we measure converts organization itself into better answers", with organisation eroding as the store grows for all but the strongest management agent — and changing the tool set alone reshaped the store as strongly as swapping the model [407]. **Moderate** (preprint, credible group). This converges with three independent lines: HippoRAG 2's authors note earlier structured-RAG approaches drop "considerably below standard RAG" on basic factual memory [408]; Microsoft's GraphRAG headline is LLM-judged comprehensiveness and diversity win-rates on global sensemaking, reporting no retrieval-accuracy comparison [303]; a systematic RAG-vs-GraphRAG evaluation finds neither dominates and hybrids win [300]; and the independent LOCOMO testbed ranks both graph systems last [402]. **Strong** as a convergent negative for the accuracy case. Structure remains defensible on cost, latency and auditability. A related measurement discipline follows from the observation that retrieval recall and end-answer accuracy diverge — MemConflict reports "answer correctness often diverging from memory retrieval and ranking" across six long-term memory systems [409] (**weak**, preprint, **[unverified pass]**) — so instrument the two halves separately: did the right evidence get retrieved, and given the right evidence was the answer right.

Three findings that are load-bearing for a knowledge-context system but rest on thin evidence, all **[unverified pass]**: recency was the *worst* retention signal in the one head-to-head comparison found (0.368 gold-evidence retention against 0.770 for a learned multi-factor model, 0.657 uniform, 0.518 best single factor; 479 cases, paired bootstrap CIs above zero) with reliability and user-relevance dominating the learned weights [410], corroborated only by a second preprint observing time-driven decay collapsing to near-zero weights over 100 rounds [411] — **weak**; belief-based (Bayesian) conflict resolution gives minimal advantage over last-write-wins on ordinary conversational recall because standard benchmarks contain almost no differently-reliable conflicting evidence, while grounding trust in *source provenance* resists volumetric poisoning where grounding it in the statement's textual confidence does not [405] — **weak**, though it reports against its own thesis; and memory-substrate rankings do not transfer across operating regimes, with broad retrieval improving long-context factual QA while *degrading* sequential decision-making [412] — **moderate** for the substrate study, **weak** for the observed inversion of architecture rankings between three and nine weeks of history (n=6 users, p=0.031) [413].

Two findings deserve to survive into the design regardless of the memory-system question. First, retrieval can be net-negative: on RAGuard, whose misleading documents are naturally occurring (drawn from Reddit) rather than synthetically injected, *every* tested LLM-powered RAG system performed worse than its own zero-shot no-retrieval baseline while human annotators did better, and the authors argue that benchmarks using gold documents or synthetic noise systematically overestimate RAG performance [414]. **Strong** (NeurIPS 2025). A no-retrieval arm belongs in every evaluation. Second, **memory consolidation strips authorisation**: "authority collapse", where converting interaction history into reusable facts preserves the claim but erases the source constraints governing its authorised use, was found in 48 of 49 configurations across seven consolidators and seven backbones, and persisting predicted authority labels through consolidation drove unauthorised-action rates from 16.9% to 0.0% with no loss of task performance [415]. **Moderate** (broad configuration coverage, clean intervention, not peer-reviewed or replicated). This is the index-time half of permission enforcement that query-time filtering silently depends on: any summarisation step that does not propagate provenance and authority is a security defect, not a lossy convenience. Independently, no current method achieves utility, access control and reliable forgetting simultaneously in a multi-principal shared store — and long-context prompting often scores *best* on governance, at high token cost, while retrieval and external-memory methods leak unauthorised and explicitly deleted content [416]. **Moderate**.

Finally, a reason to store observations rather than conclusions: multi-turn interaction degrades performance by 39% on average across six generation tasks versus fully-specified single-turn, decomposing into a small aptitude drop plus a large rise in unreliability, because models commit early and cannot recover [417]. **Moderate** (widely cited preprint, not peer-reviewed). A memory layer that persists early conclusions converts a recoverable in-context mistake into a durable stored one.

### Provenance: what W3C PROV and Web Annotation specify, and what they cost

PROV is explicitly a three-tier vocabulary designed to be subsetted, and the tiers differ wildly in size: Starting Point is 3 classes + 10 properties, Expanded 7 + 16, Qualified 20 + 20; the spec itself says users "may only need to use parts of the entire ontology" [245], and PROV-DM likewise separates core structures (Entity/Activity, Derivation, Agent) from extended ones (Bundles, Alternates, Collections) [418]. **Strong** (Recommendation text). "Adopt PROV" is therefore not one decision but at least three.

Does anyone use the full model? W3C's own implementation census is the direct answer: of 66 reported implementations, **only three both produced and consumed all PROV-DM constructs**, and only three validators implemented all of PROV-CONSTRAINTS; over 40 touched PROV-O, 12 implemented PROV-N, and 13 of 66 entries were vocabulary *extensions* [419]. **Strong** (primary census). The cost is structural and countable: the unqualified form (`:e1 prov:wasGeneratedBy :a1`) is 2 triples, the qualified form adds 3 triples plus a new node before any domain property attaches, and roughly half of PROV-O (20 of ~30 classes, 20 of ~46 properties) exists solely to support that pattern [245]. **Strong.** Any knowledge-context system needs per-edge attributes — confidence, role, timestamp, extraction method — so this is the pattern it would be paying for, at ~2.5× graph blow-up per annotated edge. Emitting PROV from application code was itself hard enough to motivate a standardised templating layer that splits provenance topology from runtime values, with bindings measured at ~40% of the size of the provenance they expand into [420]. **Moderate** (IEEE TSE, abstract).

The four largest real-world provenance deployments do not use PROV-O as a wire format:

| Deployment | Model actually used | Relation to PROV | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|
| C2PA content credentials [421] | CBOR/COSE/JUMBF manifests: claims, assertions, ingredients | A direct token count over the 2.1 spec finds **zero** occurrences of "PROV-O", "prov-dm", "W3C PROV" or "RDF"; it normatively references Dublin Core (dc:format in claims), schema.org, XMP, Exif, IPTC | strong |
| OpenLineage [422] | Own Job / Run / Dataset + facets JSON model | none | strong |
| HL7 FHIR [423] | Bespoke `Provenance` resource "based on the W3C Provenance specification" | Covers only PROV's Generation-of-Entity; Usage and all other Activity are routed to `AuditEvent` | strong |
| RO-Crate [424] | schema.org in JSON-LD | A full W3C PROV bundle is permitted only as an attached side-file, because full PROV is "at a different granularity level that might become overwhelming if represented directly" | strong |

Where PROV *is* deployed as PROV it is scientific-workflow provenance, always with domain extensions layered on (CWLProv, wfprov/wfdesc, ProvONE) [425] — a good fit for an ingestion pipeline's execution trace, a poor fit for editorial provenance of knowledge claims. **Moderate.** The defensible synthesis: PROV's durable value is its *conceptual* vocabulary (entity/activity/agent; generation, usage, derivation, attribution, revision). Name fields after PROV concepts so the mapping is documentable; store records.

Vocabulary size and vocabulary *use* are almost unrelated, and this is measured at web scale. schema.org V30.0 has 823 types and 1,529 properties [426]; Web Data Commons' October 2024 corpus (2.39B pages parsed, 16.5M domains carrying structured data, 74.0B triples) shows head classes on millions of pay-level domains — SearchAction 6.76M, Person 5.57M, Organization 4.32M — against tail classes on hundreds or dozens: ClaimReview 343, Park 337, TelevisionStation 89, RiverBodyOfWater 25 [427]. Four to five orders of magnitude, inside one vocabulary. **Strong.** Adding terms demonstrably does not produce data in them: Paulheim's diachronic analysis found domains "for which no deployed data can be found at all, such as the medical domain, where a larger vocabulary was bulk-integrated", an official extension mechanism "only rarely used, without a measurable impact", and `schema:sameAs` — the one property that would make the corpus linkable — "deployed by less than 0.02% of all Microdata providers", while deprecations propagate quickly [428]. **Strong** (web-scale corpus, read in full). Dublin Core repeats the pattern: OAI-PMH §3.4 normatively mandates unqualified 15-element Dublin Core as the interoperability floor rather than DCMI Terms' 55+ properties [429][430], and across 35 harvested collections only `title` and `identifier` appear in 100% of repositories, with `creator` in 39% of *records* and `rights` in 52% [431]. **Strong.** The engineering consequence is blunt: never let ranking, permission enforcement or conflict resolution depend on a field that is 40% populated — the rule will silently apply to a minority of the corpus. Crosswalks compound this: remapping native records to Dublin Core measurably changes description/format/subject/type counts, with multi-valued content collapsed into single elements with ad-hoc delimiters, and published crosswalks "do not always account for semantic values of elements" [431] — so retain the raw source record beside the normalised one, or you can never distinguish "the source did not say" from "our mapping dropped it".

What *did* drive schema.org adoption, empirically, was not extensibility: direct business incentive (better search listings produced stricter schema conformance), ready-to-adapt documentation examples, implementation inside widely deployed CMSs (producing large-scale usage "sometimes even unconscious to the website owner" *and* greater homogeneity), and a standard flexible enough to legalise systematic violations after observing them [428]. **Moderate.** Deployed-markup error rates are correspondingly low — 5.6% of documents using undefined types and 9.7% undefined properties, against 38.8% and 72.4% in Linked Open Data [432] (**moderate**, seen via citation only). Auto-emission at ingestion is what produces both the volume and the consistency.

The W3C Web Annotation Data Model is the better-specified primitive for the citation anchor itself. An Annotation must have exactly one IRI and one or more targets; a target may be a `SpecificResource` bearing `FragmentSelector`, `TextQuoteSelector` (exact + prefix + suffix), `TextPositionSelector` (start/end), `RangeSelector`, `CSSSelector`, `XPathSelector` or `SVGSelector`, and "A Selector MAY be refinedBy 1 or more other Selectors"; it carries `creator`/`created` *and* `generator`/`generated` — the human-versus-machine distinction an AI system needs — plus a `motivation` from a 13-value extensible list [433]. **Strong** (Recommendation, read in full). It is genuinely deployed, and deployments both constrain and extend it: IIIF Presentation API 3.0 restricts `motivation` to painting/supplementing and deviates on `label` [434], while a live Hypothesis API payload carries **three co-registered selectors for one target** — a RangeSelector (XPath containers + offsets), a TextPositionSelector (char 68381–68472) and a TextQuoteSelector with prefix and suffix context [435]. **Strong** for the observation; note that whether multi-selector anchoring measurably improves re-anchoring survival under drift is **unmeasured** — the practice is deployed, the rationale is obvious, and no published evaluation quantifies it.

Content drift, not link rot, is the failure that matters. Klein et al. analysed 3.5M+ articles and 1M+ web references and found "one out of five STM articles suffering from reference rot", rising to seven in ten among articles that actually contain web references; the decisive detail is that "the number of articles infected with reference rot is very close to the number of articles that lack appropriate Mementos", with only 22% of references having an archive snapshot within 30 days of publication and 0.3% within one day [436]. **Strong** (PLOS ONE). A 200 response is not evidence the content still supports the claim, and no third-party archive can be assumed to exist: capture and content-hash the cited extract at index time and store the retrieval datetime. If cached context is versioned, RFC 7089 (Memento) is the solved interface shape — Original Resource / Memento / TimeGate / TimeMap, negotiated via `Accept-Datetime` [437]. **Strong.**

For conflict, Wikidata's deployed model is a three-part design worth copying wholesale: three ranks (preferred / normal / deprecated) where deprecation is *retention with a marker*, kept explicitly to prevent accidental re-addition and to document how knowledge evolved; rank orthogonal to sourcing — "References merely state where a data value comes from; ranks indicate what data value is considered the most correct"; and queries returning best rank silently while the conflict stays inspectable [243]. **Strong** (primary documentation of a large production knowledge base). Conflating "which value wins" with "where the value came from" into one field is the standard design error. Note also that RDF has no native way to attach provenance to an individual statement; the clean primitive (`rdf:reifies`, triple terms, motivated in the spec by wanting to "make statements about unasserted statements") is still only at Candidate Recommendation as of April 2026 [246][438] — **moderate**, and a concrete argument for a record store over a triple store when per-claim provenance and per-claim conflict are the central requirement.

### Are cited answers faithful to their citations? No, and it replicates

Two independent human audits of production systems, two years apart, agree:

| Audit | Systems | Finding | Grade |
|---|---|---|---|
| Liu, Zhang & Liang [439] | Bing Chat, NeevaAI, perplexity.ai, YouChat | "a mere 51.5% of generated sentences are fully supported by citations and only 74.5% of citations support their associated sentence" | strong (Findings of EMNLP 2023, human annotation) |
| Venkit et al. [440] | You.com, Bing Copilot, Perplexity; 303 queries + 21-participant usability study | ~25–30% of statements unsupported by listed sources; roughly two-thirds of citations point to a supporting source for You.com/Bing and **under half** for Perplexity; Bing lists 36% uncited sources; all engines include ≥30% of sources supporting no unique information; citation thoroughness 20–25% | strong for the automated audit, moderate for the usability findings (preprint) |
| RAGTruth [174] | ~18,000 naturally generated RAG responses, word-level annotation | Exists precisely because "despite the integration of RAG, LLMs may still present unsupported or contradictory claims to the retrieved contents"; a fine-tuned small model matches prompt-based GPT-4 at detecting them | moderate (ACL 2024, abstract) |

Mis-citation is a *distinct* failure from ungroundedness: "even when there exists a source that supports a statement, all engines still frequently cite a different incorrect source" [440]. Users do not compensate — one participant: "I mean it's just like you see a citation, you assume it's a valid source... I'll just see that there is a source. That's it. I don't verify it." **Moderate.** If only one check is built, build the per-citation one.

Attribution as formally defined is explicitly *not* a truth claim. AIS asks whether a hearer would judge "According to P, s" to hold, "where s is interpreted relative to time t"; its authors state that source characteristics "such as source quality... can be measured using complementary metrics that are out of scope in this work", and that "AIS does not set policy about where model output should fall" [441]. **Strong.** A system can be 100% attributable and 100% wrong; rendering "cited" as "verified" is the most consequential elision in this area. Because attribution is indexed to time t, every attribution assertion needs a timestamp and re-verification when the source changes.

Automatic attribution evaluation is a usable *aggregate* signal and an unreliable per-example gate. ALCE's TRUE-NLI-based citation metrics agree with human judgement at Cohen's κ = 0.698 for citation recall and only 0.525 for precision (85.1% / 77.6% accuracy) [442]; AttributionBench reports a fine-tuned GPT-3.5 reaching only ~80% macro-F1 on the binary supported/unsupported formulation, with most errors on nuanced information and on the information gap between model and annotator [443]; SummEdits finds most LLMs "close to random chance" on inconsistency detection, with GPT-4 8% below estimated human performance [444]. **Strong** as a convergent limit. There is also no metric for the middle state: ALCE's authors state outright that "there is no existing model that can judge whether a citation partially supports a claim" and that prompting for the three-way judgement "yields poor results" [442], and a dedicated comparative study of full/partial/no-support metrics concluded "no single metric consistently excels across all evaluations" [445]. **Moderate.** A schema with only cited/not-cited collapses a three-state fact and systematically penalises correct multi-source synthesis.

Citation-quality metrics have a degenerate optimum. On ASQA, emitting the top-1 retrieved passage verbatim scores citation recall 99.4 and precision 99.4 against ChatGPT's 73.6 / 63.0 — while MAUVE fluency collapses from 66.6 to 20.8 and answer correctness drops from 40.4 to 35.1 [442]. **Strong** (published ablation). Citation quality reported alone is maximised by copying; it must always be paired with a measure that copying destroys.

Two constructive results. Architecturally, "Attribute First, then Generate" restructures generation into content selection → sentence planning → sequential generation so that the selected source segments *are* the attributions, reporting more concise citations, maintained or improved generation quality and attribution accuracy, and significantly reduced human fact-verification time [446]. **Moderate** (ACL 2024, abstract) — but it is the highest-leverage decision available, because it eliminates mis-citation by construction rather than auditing for it. Evaluation-wise, the method with the strongest validation path is nugget-based: TREC's AutoNuggetizer shows "strong agreement at the run level between scores derived from fully automatic nugget evaluation and human-based variants", while being explicit that "further research is necessary... particularly in establishing robust per-topic agreement" [447]; the companion SIGIR perspective pairs nuggets for completeness with separate citation checks for verifiability [448]. **Moderate**, with a hard boundary: the validated unit is the *run*, not the answer. Nugget scores compare versions of a system; they do not gate a response. Human judgement does not rescue per-answer gating either — AIS inter-annotator agreement runs from Krippendorff's α = 0.91 on conversational QA down to 0.60 (Wizard-of-Wikipedia) and 0.46 (CNN/DM summarization), which the authors attribute to how much context an annotator must read, at 73–308 seconds per item after training (687s at pilot) [441]. **Strong.** Cite small units, and price human review at 1–5 minutes per claim before promising it at volume. The vocabulary itself is fragmented — a systematic analysis of 134 papers found 300 evaluation metrics across seven dimensions [449] — so definitions have to be written down rather than assumed.

### Abstention and calibration: selective prediction, risk-coverage, and where confidence comes from

The classical framing is the one to adopt as vocabulary: Chow's optimum error-reject trade-off [450], El-Yaniv & Wiener's selective classification with explicit risk-coverage characterisations [451], and Geifman & El-Yaniv's deep-network selective classifier guaranteeing a target risk at chosen coverage [452], with a TACL survey organising LLM abstention along query / model / human-values axes [453]. **Strong.** Using *coverage*, *selective risk*, *risk-coverage curve* and *coverage at target risk* makes an evaluation section directly comparable to published baselines.

Two methodological results should be adopted before any gate is built. First, the standard evaluation practice for failure detection is broken: a holistic reformulation measuring risk-coverage (AURC) across the full spectrum of shifts rather than AUROC on curated splits "reveals a simple softmax response baseline as the overall best performing method", which the authors describe as underlining drastic shortcomings of current evaluation [454]. **Strong** (ICLR 2023 oral). Report AURC and coverage-at-target-risk, and always include max-softmax or max-retrieval-score as the baseline any proposed gate must beat. Second, plug-in ECE estimators are biased and under-report miscalibration, and popular recalibration methods (Platt, temperature scaling) are "less calibrated than reported", with current techniques unable to estimate how miscalibrated they are [455]. **Strong** (NeurIPS 2019 spotlight). Do not ship plug-in ECE as a calibration KPI.

Abstention is not free. Selective classification **magnifies group disparities**: across five vision and NLP datasets, abstention improved average accuracy while widening accuracy gaps between groups, and increasing abstention *decreased* accuracy on some groups outright, with the condition characterised by full-coverage accuracy and a left-log-concavity property of the margin distribution [456]. **Strong** (ICLR 2021, with a proof for symmetric margins). Risk-coverage must be reported per segment — tenant, topic, language, document age, source — not only in aggregate. The symmetric failure is over-abstention: XSTest systematically documents exaggerated refusal of clearly safe prompts that merely resemble unsafe ones [457], while refusal-aware instruction tuning shows the capability generalises as a meta-skill and improves calibration [458]. **Moderate** both. Every abstention-policy change needs a false-abstention suite shipped alongside the hallucination suite.

Abstention capability also does not transfer. A top SQuAD 2.0-trained system "drops considerably in out-of-domain scenarios", failing even on the simpler non-competitive IDK questions where the context contains no entity of the expected answer type [459]; SQuAD 2.0 contains learnable artifacts that MRC models demonstrably exploit [460]; and under domain shift, softmax-based abstention fares poorly because models are overconfident out of distribution, whereas a calibrator trained to predict model error — benefiting from exposure to out-of-domain data *even from a different domain than the test set* — answers 56% of questions at 80% accuracy against 48% for raw probabilities [461]. **Strong** (three peer-reviewed studies with explicit OOD splits). There is no off-the-shelf abstention capability to buy; there is a labelled in-corpus set to build, plus a held-out OOD slice that need not match production exactly.

### Do retrieval-side signals predict answerability better than model-side confidence?

This is the section's central design question, and the honest answer is: **the useful axis is supervision, not side.**

| Predictor family | Reported strength | Grade |
|---|---|---|
| Pre-retrieval QPP (IDF, SCQ, VAR) | correlation ~0–0.1 with actual RAG gain [462] | moderate |
| Unsupervised post-retrieval score-distribution predictors (NQC / score separability [463], WIG, SMV) | ~0.1–0.2 [462]; QPP-only prediction of answer quality on NQ/BM25 Spearman ρ ≈ 0.245, rising to 0.282 with reader-side context perplexity [464] | moderate |
| Reference-list predictor | 0.25–0.30 [462] | moderate |
| Model-side post-generation uncertainty | 0.35–0.55 [462] | moderate |
| Supervised cross-encoder over question + context | 0.45–0.49 [462] | moderate |
| Learned passage-utility predictor (one small-model pass per passage, no generation) | AUROC 0.78–0.80 average across 4 QA models × 6 datasets, vs 0.70–0.78 for p(True), 0.63–0.73 for semantic entropy, 0.56–0.73 for answer perplexity; on deliberately unanswerable RefuNQ 0.78–0.81 vs 0.60–0.68 for p(True) [465] | moderate (TMLR 2025, DeLong tests) |
| Joint (question + context + both candidate answers) | 0.71–0.79 on NQ-Open, 0.87–0.89 on TriviaQA [462] | moderate |

Three conclusions follow, and none of them is "retrieval beats the model". **Raw retrieval statistics are weak** — a cosine threshold, a score gap or a score standard deviation will not carry an answerability decision. **A learned retrieval-side sufficiency signal is strong, and its margin is largest exactly in the low-coverage regime** you care about, because the model "confidently generates a response and also fails at assessing it" precisely where it lacks the knowledge [465]. And **the best gate is a combiner over both families**: combining a sufficient-context autorater label with model self-rated confidence in a simple logistic regression improved the selective-accuracy/coverage trade-off by >10% (Gemma 27B on HotpotQA, high-accuracy region) and >5% (Gemini 1.5 Pro at ~70% coverage) over confidence alone — while abstaining on the sufficient-context signal *alone lowers overall performance*, because models answer a substantial fraction correctly even with insufficient context [208]. **Moderate** (ICLR 2025, 3 datasets × 5 model families). The gain disappears when overall accuracy is very low.

The confound is worth stating explicitly, because it is the main reason not to over-read the table: every strong "retrieval-side" result is a model *trained on end-task correctness*, while the model-side baselines it beats (perplexity, entropy, p(True)) are unsupervised. Nobody has run the matched-supervision ablation. **Unresolved.** Two secondary asymmetries cut in opposite directions and are worth measuring locally: retrieval-side predictor accuracy *declines* as k grows (2→10) while post-generation answer perplexity *improves* with k [464]. A related caution: semantic entropy — entropy over meaning-equivalence clusters — is the right form of model-side uncertainty for free-form QA in the closed-book setting [466], but its advantage narrows once retrieval is in the loop, where it is outperformed by both p(True) and the single-pass passage-utility predictor while costing N generations per query [465]. **Moderate.**

A finding that constrains the architecture rather than the features: **injecting retrieved context suppresses the reader's own abstention regardless of sufficiency.** Measured abstention rates collapse from 100% → 18.6% (Gemini 1.5 Pro), 84.1% → 52% (Claude 3.5 Sonnet) and 34.4% → 31.2% (GPT-4o) when RAG context is added, and given *sufficient* context models still hallucinate more often than they abstain across all three datasets tested [208]; "negative rejection" is among the weakest of four measured RAG abilities on RGB [467]; and on CRAG, straightforward RAG lifts accuracy only from ≤34% to 44%, with the best industry solutions answering 63% without hallucination [468]. **Moderate to strong.** The abstention decision must therefore live *outside* the generator — prompting the reader to say "I don't know if the context is insufficient" is measurably undermined by the act of supplying context.

Retrieval-side thresholds are fitted parameters, not constants. QPP methods' relative prediction quality flips depending on which of several equivalent query formulations represents the same information need [469]; QPP quality drops statistically significantly on neural retrievers versus bag-of-words — by as much as 10% on passage retrieval — and fails hardest on exactly the queries where neural rankers differ most from lexical ones [470]; and correlation-based point-estimate evaluation of QPP is itself statistically unsound and should be modelled as a distribution [471]. **Strong.** Any threshold needs re-calibration per retriever, per corpus, per k, with a regression test that fires when the operating point drifts.

Retrieval *relevance* is also not answer utility. The retriever's highest-scoring documents that are not directly relevant actively hurt LLM accuracy, while adding **random** documents improved accuracy by up to 35% [472]; position in the assembled context can dominate relevance [169]; query variants maximising nDCG often fail to produce the best generated answers [473]; and a retrieval signal deliberately aligned with answer support "does not robustly predict answer quality on unseen topics" [474]. **Strong** for the first two, **weak** (2026 preprints, **[unverified pass]**) for the last two. Never use nDCG or recall@k as the proxy target for an answerability gate; train on end-task correctness.

On **staleness metadata** specifically — the third retrieval-side signal in the design question — the evidence is indirect and there is a real gap. Outdated content in the corpus degrades RAG two ways: it distracts the model from correct information *and* misleads it into potentially harmful outputs **even when current information is also available** [194] (**moderate**, 2025 preprint benchmark, **[unverified pass]**); CRAG shows accuracy markedly lower for facts with higher temporal dynamism [468]; SituatedQA finds ~16.5% of NQ-Open questions have temporally or geographically context-dependent answers, with models trained on past data dropping ~15 points on present-day questions *even with an updated evidence corpus* [475]; and time-aware training improves calibration on facts from future periods [200]. Conversely a model's *claimed* knowledge cutoff is unreliable — measured "effective cutoffs often differ from reported cutoffs" [476] — so freshness must be a document-level attribute you own and can audit, never something the reader arbitrates from priors. **But no study isolates staleness metadata as a feature and reports its incremental AUROC once retrieval-sufficiency and model-confidence features are already in the model.** That is an open question, not a supported claim.

Two structural points close the loop with the provenance section. First, permissions and validity intervals must be enforced *inside* query processing, not as post-filters: Büttcher & Clarke show constructively that with a system-wide index, BM25 ranking over global statistics and post-filtering, an unprivileged user can create a few files containing novel terms, read back the scores, solve algebraically for |F| and avgdl, and recover the exact document frequency of any term including in unreadable files — and that query-integration is also *faster* when the visible fraction is small, "because relevance scores for fewer documents have to be computed" [477]. **Strong** (USENIX FAST 2005, constructive attack plus evaluation). Whether this transfers to dense vector retrieval is **unresolved**: the attack depends on collection-level terms in the score, and while ANN index structure, score normalisation, MMR diversification and hybrid BM25 components all reintroduce corpus dependence, no published study measures leakage from permission-post-filtered vector search. Second, filtered retrieval is not free: filter-unaware ANN indices give "high search latency or low recall" on filtered queries [478], predicate-subgraph traversal over HNSW yields 2–1,000× higher throughput at fixed recall versus prior hybrid methods [363], and policy-aware vector search is framed as an open three-way tension between policy correctness, recall and latency with no published quantification of pre-filter vs post-filter vs partitioned-index trade-offs [366]. **Strong** for the systems results, **weak** for the policy framing. ACLs and validity intervals are index-native schema decisions made *before* the index exists.

Finally, two framings worth adopting wholesale. Model the system as **two gates, not one** — retrieve-or-not and answer-or-abstain — since they share features but have different targets and costs: retrieval-augmented LMs outperform much larger unassisted models on low-popularity entities while unassisted models stay competitive on high-popularity ones, and retrieving only when necessary improves performance *and* reduces cost [137][479][462]. **Moderate.** And adopt an **asymmetric primary KPI** (wrong ≪ abstain < correct) from day one, with "knowledge-gap canaries" — questions whose answers are verifiably absent from your corpus — as a standing regression suite: CRAG operationalises the scoring (+1 / −1 / 0) [468], the argued diagnosis is that benchmarks scoring abstention like a wrong answer create pressure toward guessing [480], and a penalty-aware audit of three commercial RAG products found accuracy-when-answering clustered at 97.0–98.0% while canary-violation rates differed roughly sixfold (16.7% vs 98.1%) — conventional accuracy discriminated almost nothing about the property that matters [481]. **Moderate**, and the audit is a 2026 industry preprint **[unverified pass]**.

Conformal prediction does not rescue any of this at the individual level. Exact conditional coverage is provably impossible in a distribution-free setting [482]; RAG-specific conformal frameworks inherit marginal-only coverage; and one recent system explicitly reports that after conformal chunk selection "the associated statistical guarantees do not hold generally, since the assumption of sample exchangeability depends on the retriever setup", shipping diagnostics for whether a setup qualifies [483]. **Strong.** A "90% guarantee" is a population average that protects no particular tenant, topic, jurisdiction or freshness stratum — and the calibration-sample cost of per-segment risk control is uncharacterised.

### What the evidence does not support

**"A big enough context window makes a memory system unnecessary."** Refuted for unbounded histories by a within-model ablation: the *same* model loses 30.3% (GPT-4o) to 55.1% (Llama-3.1-70B) relative accuracy when given a full 115k-token history rather than just the evidence sessions [389], reproduced independently at ~80% → ~55% for Claude Sonnet 4 [390]. But the claim is largely *correct* for bounded corpora that fit: long context beats RAG on average when sufficiently resourced [95] and rivals dedicated retrieval pipelines on LOFT [392]. The real variable is corpus boundedness, not window size.

**"Lost in the middle is a live constraint — put important content at the start or end."** Largely obsolete as stated. LongPiBench, over five commercial and six open-source models, reports that "most current models are robust against the 'lost in the middle' issue"; the bias that survives is driven by the *distance between* multiple relevant pieces, not absolute position [396]. The original TACL finding was real for its model generation [169]. This one is **contested** rather than settled — the counter-evidence is one benchmark, and the mitigation is cheap — but it should not be cited as current fact.

**"A model that scores well on needle-in-a-haystack can use its full advertised context window."** Contradicted three ways: most long-context models achieve perfect NIAH while showing large downstream gaps, and HELMET's own categories correlate poorly with each other [393]; only about half of models claiming ≥32K maintain satisfactory performance at 32K [394]; and stripping lexical overlap between question and needle drops 11 of 13 models claiming 128K+ below half their short-context baseline at 32K, GPT-4o from 99.3% to 69.7% [395]. NIAH is a lexical-match task that rewards the shortcut it fails to test.

**"Commercial memory layers substantially outperform plain retrieval."** An independent reproducible LOCOMO testbed found mem0, plain RAG and full-context all clustered at 77–81% with Graphiti and cognee at 55–56% — inverting the vendor ordering — and plain RAG matching the top cluster at **8.4× lower total cost of ownership** than mem0 [402]. Separately, Letta reported being unable to reproduce Mem0's MemGPT baseline (Mem0 did not respond to requests for clarification or supply the modified implementation) and measured 74.0% where Mem0 reported 68.5% [404][403].

**"Memory systems save >90% of token cost versus resubmitting the conversation."** Conditional at best. An independent serving-cost study found the break-even point ranges "from the first tens of turns for the cheapest to never within 400 turns for the most expensive", that the backbone drives cost as much as the memory system does, and that serving cost cannot be predicted from conversation length and message size alone [401][403]. Write-side extraction and consolidation calls — which scale with ingestion, not queries — are the cost vendor figures omit.

**"MemGPT / virtual context management is an established, peer-reviewed result."** It is an arXiv preprint (v2, February 2024) with no peer-reviewed venue despite ~1,195 citations, and its evaluation used Deep Memory Retrieval, a benchmark defined by the same team and now near-saturated (a competitor reports 94.8% against MemGPT's 93.4%) [397][398]. Cite the idea; do not cite the result as established.

**"LOCOMO scores are comparable across papers, so you can rank systems from published numbers."** Independent evaluations place memory systems at 21–54% where system papers self-report 68–99% on the same benchmark; six unreported protocol variables move rankings, and one convincing win vanished once retrieval budget was equalised — the winner had been pulling 2.6M characters per query [401][400]. Token-F1 and LLM-as-judge differ by 27.5 points on identical outputs [405], and changing only the credited scoring target moves nDCG on 83.4–94.0% of queries without rerunning retrieval [406].

**"Structuring memory as a knowledge graph or formal ontology improves answer quality."** Converging evidence against from four directions: organisation roughly halves retrieval cost but "no agent we measure converts organization itself into better answers" [407]; earlier KG-structured RAG "drops considerably below standard RAG" on basic factual memory [408]; GraphRAG's headline is LLM-judged comprehensiveness/diversity win-rates on global sensemaking with no retrieval-accuracy comparison reported [303]; and the independent LOCOMO testbed ranks both graph systems last, attributing the gap to retrieval incompleteness [402][300]. Structure is defensible on cost, latency and auditability. The accuracy case is not made.

**"Expire memories by recency; decay them with time."** Recency was the *worst* retention signal in the one head-to-head comparison available: 0.368 gold-evidence retention against 0.770 for a learned multi-factor model, 0.657 uniform and 0.518 for the best single factor, with reliability and self/user relevance dominating the learned weights and query-time goal similarity correctly down-weighted [410]; a separate study observed purely time-driven decay collapsing to near-zero weights over 100 rounds [411]. Both are unreplicated preprints from an unverified review pass — the claim they refute is popular and the refutation is thin, so treat this as "the obvious default is unsupported", not "the opposite is proven".

**"The Generative Agents retrieval score (recency + importance + relevance) is a validated design."** It was validated against human ratings of the *believability* of simulated agent behaviour in a sandbox, with an ablation showing observation, planning and reflection each contribute to believability [484]. There is no evaluation of factual retrieval accuracy in that work. Combined with the recency result above, the formula's most-copied term is the one with the worst independent evidence.

**"LLM-as-a-judge is an acceptable objective metric for memory-system accuracy."** The ~80% judge-human agreement figure was measured on *chatbot response preference*, not factual memory recall, and the same paper documents position, verbosity and self-enhancement biases [121]. On memory benchmarks specifically, judge and token-F1 diverge by 27.5 points on identical outputs [405] and judge choice is one of the protocol variables shown to move rankings [400]. **Provenance failure: no study validating judge-human agreement on an agent-memory benchmark was found.** Every published memory-system number rests on a metric that has not been validated for the task it is being used on.

**"Adding retrieval grounds the model, so it can only help."** On RAGuard, whose misleading documents are naturally occurring rather than synthetically injected, *every* tested RAG system performed worse than its own zero-shot no-retrieval baseline while human annotators did better; the authors argue that benchmarks using gold documents or synthetic noise systematically overestimate RAG performance [414]. Broader retrieval is also not uniformly safer — it improves long-context factual QA while *degrading* sequential decision-making by diverting attention from action-critical information [412].

**"RAG makes models more willing to say 'I don't know'."** Contradicted. Adding retrieved context collapses abstention: Gemini 1.5 Pro 100% → 18.6%, Claude 3.5 Sonnet 84.1% → 52%; and with *sufficient* context models still hallucinate more often than they abstain [208]. "Negative rejection" is among the weakest of four measured RAG abilities on RGB [467], and CRAG lifts straightforward-RAG accuracy only from ≤34% to 44% [468].

**"Frontier models know when to say 'I don't know'."** Larger, higher-baseline models "excel at answering queries when the context is sufficient, but often output incorrect answers instead of abstaining when the context is not" [208]; GPT-4o-class models adopt incorrect retrieved content over their own correct prior more than 60% of the time, and the *less* confident the model is in its prior, the *more* likely it is to adopt the retrieved content [181]; SelfAware reports a "considerable gap between the capabilities of these models and human proficiency in recognizing the limits of their knowledge" across 20 LLMs [485].

**"LLMs are badly calibrated."** Over-broad as stated. Large models are well calibrated on multiple-choice and true/false questions "when they are provided in the right format" [486]; pre-trained GPT-4 is described by its own technical report as "highly calibrated" on MMLU [487]; BERT/RoBERTa are calibrated in-domain [488]; and the decay-with-size and decay-with-shift trends were revisited, with the most recent non-convolutional image models among the best calibrated [489]. Miscalibration is regime-specific — free-form generation, out-of-distribution inputs, post-RLHF models — and heavily format-dependent.
