The ECE numbers underpinning much of this literature are themselves biased downward [455].

**"RLHF destroys calibration." — Provenance failure.** This traces almost entirely to Figure 8 of the OpenAI GPT-4 Technical Report: "the pre-trained model is highly calibrated... after the post-training process, the calibration is reduced" [487]. That is one vendor figure, one model, one multiple-choice benchmark (a subset of MMLU), with no disclosed methodology, no error bars and no released data. No peer-reviewed multi-model replication was found. The nearest independent support is indirect — for RLHF-fine-tuned models, verbalized confidences are typically better calibrated than conditional probabilities [490] — which is consistent with, but does not establish, the general law. Treat it as an unreplicated vendor observation, not a design premise.

**"Just ask the model for a confidence number."** Contested. A fine-tuned GPT-3 can emit well-calibrated verbalized probabilities [491], and verbalized confidences beat conditional probabilities for RLHF'd models [490] — but a systematic evaluation of confidence elicitation found LLMs verbalizing confidence "tend to be overconfident, potentially imitating human patterns" [492]. Use it as one candidate feature in a trained combiner, never as a probability you threshold directly.

**"A retrieval similarity score threshold tells you whether the corpus can answer the question."** Contradicted quantitatively: score-distribution predictors (NQC/score separability, WIG, SMV) correlate ~0.1–0.2 with actual RAG gain and pre-retrieval predictors ~0–0.1, against 0.35–0.55 for model uncertainty and 0.71–0.89 for joint predictors [462]; QPP-only prediction of answer quality tops out around ρ ≈ 0.25 [464]. The thresholds also do not transfer across retrievers, query formulations or k [470][469][471].

**"Retrieval-side signals are inherently more trustworthy than model confidence because they are grounded."** Half-true, and the useful distinction is *supervision*, not side. Raw retrieval statistics are weak; learned retrieval-side scorers are strong (AUROC 0.78–0.81, beating p(True) and semantic entropy, especially on unanswerable questions) [465]; but retrieval-side accuracy declines as context grows while model-side post-generation perplexity improves [464], the strongest predictors in every study are *joint* [462], and abstaining on a retrieval-sufficiency signal alone measurably lowers overall performance [208]. **Provenance failure: the comparison is confounded — every strong retrieval-side result is supervised on end-task correctness while the model-side baselines it beats are unsupervised, and no matched-supervision ablation exists.**

**"Better retrieval (higher nDCG/recall) means better answers."** Contradicted. The retriever's highest-scoring documents that are not directly relevant negatively impact the LLM, while adding *random* documents improved accuracy by up to 35% [472]; position in the assembled context can matter more than relevance [169]; query variants maximising nDCG often fail to produce the best answers [473]; and an answer-support-aligned retrieval signal still "does not robustly predict answer quality on unseen topics" [474].

**"AUROC is the metric for evaluating an abstention signal."** Contradicted. A systematic re-evaluation of failure detection under a holistic risk-coverage view found many published gains to be evaluation artifacts and "a simple softmax response baseline" to be the overall best performing method, which the authors describe as underlining "the drastic shortcomings of current evaluation" [454]. Report AURC and coverage-at-target-risk alongside a max-score baseline.

**"Abstention is safe — the worst case is answering fewer questions."** Contradicted twice over. Selective classification can *magnify* accuracy disparities between groups, and increasing abstention can *decrease* accuracy on some groups outright, observed across five vision and NLP datasets with a proof of when it happens [456]. And over-abstention is a real regression: XSTest documents systematic exaggerated refusal of clearly safe prompts that merely resemble unsafe ones [457].

**"SQuAD 2.0 measures whether a system knows what it doesn't know, so a good score transfers."** Contradicted. A top SQuAD 2.0-trained system "drops considerably in out-of-domain scenarios", failing even on the simpler non-competitive IDK questions [459]; SQuAD 2.0 contains artifacts MRC models demonstrably learn [460]; and under domain shift softmax-based abstention fares poorly because models are overconfident out of distribution [461].

**"Conformal prediction gives a per-answer correctness guarantee."** Contradicted. Conformal methods provide *marginal* coverage, and exact conditional coverage is provably impossible without distributional assumptions [482]; RAG-specific frameworks inherit this, and the exchangeability assumption "depends on the retriever setup" and can simply fail, which one recent system acknowledges by shipping diagnostics for whether a given setup qualifies [483].

**"W3C PROV is *the* provenance standard, so adopt PROV-O."** Not supported as a *serialisation* choice, and the counter-evidence includes W3C's own census: of 66 reported implementations only three both produced and consumed all PROV-DM constructs, and only three validators implemented all of PROV-CONSTRAINTS [419]. The largest 2020s deployments avoid it — a direct token count over the C2PA 2.1 specification finds **zero** occurrences of "PROV-O", "prov-dm", "W3C PROV" or "RDF" while it normatively cites Dublin Core and references schema.org, XMP, Exif and IPTC [421]; OpenLineage defines its own Job/Run/Dataset+facets JSON model [422]; FHIR ships a bespoke resource "based on" PROV that covers only Generation-of-Entity [423]; RO-Crate chose schema.org in JSON-LD and permits a PROV bundle only as a side-file [424]. PROV's *concepts* are widely reused; PROV-O as a wire format is not.

**"PROV-O is cheap to adopt — it's just a vocabulary, you add some triples."** The half that carries the interesting information is expensive by construction: qualifying one influence relation goes from 2 triples to 5 triples plus a new node *before* any domain property attaches, and 20 of ~30 classes and 20 of ~46 properties exist solely to support that pattern [245]. Emitting PROV from application code was hard enough to motivate a whole templating system published in IEEE TSE, splitting topology from values, with bindings ~40% the size of the provenance they expand into [420].

**"Define a richer vocabulary and richer data will follow."** Directly contradicted at web scale. Bulk-integrating a large medical vocabulary into schema.org produced a domain "for which no deployed data can be found at all"; the official extension mechanism is "only rarely used, without a measurable impact"; and `schema:sameAs` — the property that makes the corpus linkable — is "deployed by less than 0.02% of all Microdata providers" [428]. Web Data Commons shows four to five orders of magnitude between head and tail classes inside one vocabulary of 823 types [427][426]. Dublin Core repeats it: only `title` and `identifier` reach 100% of repositories; `creator` appears in 39% of records [431]. Deprecations propagate quickly; additions do not.

**"Future-proof the vocabulary by making it openly extensible."** Both a young and a mature standard converge the other way. schema.org's official extension mechanism went essentially unused, with no measurable impact of extension classes later becoming standard [428]; C2PA 2.x actively *tightened*, removing the deprecated Exif, IPTC and schema.org metadata assertions, replacing them with one general metadata assertion where "c2pa.metadata only allows a fixed set of schemas & values" [421]. What drove schema.org adoption was business incentive, copy-pasteable examples, embedding the writer in widely deployed tools, and legalising observed violations after the fact — not extensibility.

**"An answer with citations is a verifiable answer."** Two independent human audits of production systems, two years apart, say otherwise: "a mere 51.5% of generated sentences are fully supported by citations and only 74.5% of citations support their associated sentence" [439]; and ~25–30% of statements unsupported by listed sources, under half of Perplexity's citations pointing to a supporting source, 36% uncited sources for Bing, and at least 30% of listed sources supporting no unique information [440]. Users treat presence of a citation as the verification — "I'll just see that there is a source. That's it. I don't verify it." RAGTruth exists precisely because RAG systems "may still present unsupported or contradictory claims to the retrieved contents" [174].

**"Attribution measures whether the answer is true."** Explicitly denied by the framework that defines attribution. AIS is source-relative — whether "According to P, s" holds at time t — and its authors state that source characteristics "such as source quality... are out of scope in this work" and that "AIS does not set policy about where model output should fall" [441]. A system can be perfectly attributable and entirely wrong.

**"An NLI model or LLM judge can gate citation correctness per answer."** Not at the per-example level. ALCE's own TRUE-NLI judge agrees with humans at κ = 0.698 on citation recall and only 0.525 on precision [442]; AttributionBench's fine-tuned GPT-3.5 reaches only ~80% macro-F1 on the binary formulation [443]; SummEdits finds most LLMs "close to random chance" on inconsistency detection with GPT-4 8% below humans [444]. And the middle state is unmeasurable: "there is no existing model that can judge whether a citation partially supports a claim" [442], with a dedicated comparative study finding "no single metric consistently excels" [445].

**"High citation precision/recall indicates a good cited-answer system."** The metric has a degenerate optimum: emitting the top-1 retrieved passage verbatim scores 99.4 citation recall and 99.4 precision on ASQA against 73.6 / 63.0 for a real generated answer, while MAUVE fluency collapses from 66.6 to 20.8 [442]. Citation quality reported alone is maximised by copying.

**"Human annotation is the reliable ground truth for attribution, so scale it with more annotators."** Only for short sources, and it is expensive. AIS inter-annotator agreement runs from Krippendorff's α = 0.91 on conversational QA to 0.60 (Wizard-of-Wikipedia) and 0.46 (CNN/DM summarization), attributed by the authors to how much context an annotator must read, at 73–308 seconds per item after training [441]. More annotators does not fix disagreement caused by source length; a smaller cited unit does.

**"Broken citations are a link-rot problem — check for 404s."** Content drift dominates and archive coverage cannot be assumed: "the number of articles infected with reference rot is very close to the number of articles that lack appropriate Mementos", with only 22% of references having an archive snapshot within 30 days of publication and 0.3% within one day [436]. A URL returning 200 may no longer contain the text that supported the claim.

**"Resolving a conflict means picking the right value and deleting the wrong one."** The largest production knowledge base handling conflict at scale does the opposite: Wikidata's "deprecated" rank is retention-with-a-marker, kept to prevent accidental re-addition and to document how knowledge evolved, with queries excluding it by default; and rank is kept orthogonal to sourcing — "References merely state where a data value comes from; ranks indicate what data value is considered the most correct" [243].

**"Give the model both conflicting sources with good provenance metadata and it will weigh them correctly."** Model behaviour under conflict is driven by rhetorical coherence and agreement with pretraining, not by stored metadata: LLMs are "highly receptive to external evidence even when that conflicts with their parametric memory, given that the external evidence is coherent and convincing", yet show "a strong confirmation bias when the external evidence contains some information that is consistent with their parametric memory" [180]; over-reliance on memorised information is measured directly [179][175]. Presentation order of conflicting evidence flips 11.4–25.2% of predictions across six open-weight models [189] (**moderate**, one biomedical domain, open-weight models only). Provenance stored beside the context does nothing; conflict must be stated *in* the context. Explicit surfacing does pay — multi-agent debate over conflicting evidence improves RAMDocs/AmbigDocs by up to 15.80 points absolute — but the underlying benchmark still yields only 32.60 exact match for a strong RAG baseline, and the gap widens as evidence imbalance grows [493].

**"Probabilistic belief updating is the right way to handle conflicting memories."** It provides minimal advantage over plain last-write-wins on ordinary conversational recall, because standard benchmarks contain almost no contradictory, differently-reliable evidence; what did matter in the same study is that grounding trust in *source provenance* resists volumetric poisoning while grounding it in the statement's textual confidence does not [405]. Single-author, unreplicated, from an unverified review pass — but it reports against its own thesis.

**"Enforcing permissions at query time is sufficient — filter the retrieval results by the caller's ACL."** Necessary but not sufficient, for two independent reasons. Consolidating interaction history into reusable facts erased source constraints in 48 of 49 configurations across seven consolidators and seven backbones, after which there is no metadata left for a query-time filter to act on; persisting authority labels through consolidation cut unauthorised actions from 16.9% to 0.0% [415]. And post-filtering a ranked list is itself an exploitable leak wherever ranking uses corpus statistics — an unprivileged user can recover the exact document frequency of any term in files they cannot read — while query-integration is also faster when the visible fraction is small [477].

**"Prompt-level guardrails and output filtering are adequate permission enforcement."** Microsoft Research, reporting from a shipped Copilot Tuning deployment, argues these defences "are fundamentally probabilistic and fail to offer robust protection", and that the required invariant is deterministic and participant-aware: content must be authorised for *all* users in the interaction — the **intersection** of participants' permissions, not the union [380]. GateMem separately finds retrieval and external-memory methods still leaking unauthorised and explicitly deleted content [416].

**"Permission and freshness filtering is a cheap post-processing step over search results."** Contradicted on security [477] and on performance: filter-unaware ANN indices show "high search latency or low recall" on filtered queries [478], prior hybrid-search methods either perform poorly or support only restricted predicates [363], and post-filtering an ANN result set forces over-fetching, so the cheap option is not cheap.

**Provenance failure — claims with no primary source behind them.** Four in this area deserve naming. (1) *No study measures which PROV-O terms are actually used across a corpus of published PROV documents* — there is no PROV equivalent of the Web Data Commons per-class analysis; the W3C implementation report's "3 of 66" is the closest proxy and it counts implementations, not emitted data [419]. (2) *No neutral adoption census compares OpenLineage against Apache Atlas, DataHub, Egeria or PROV* — each project publishes its own integration list, so relative deployment claims are unsupported. (3) *The canonical statement of schema.org's design philosophy (Guha, Brickley & Macbeth, ACM Queue / CACM 2016) was not retrievable* during the source review — ACM returned 403 on both the Queue page and the OA gateway — so its widely-quoted adoption figures are unverified here and no numeric claim is made from it [494]. (4) *No membership-inference study, pre/post-cutoff comparison or canary analysis exists for LOCOMO*, so **the popular assertion that LOCOMO is contaminated is itself unsupported** — what is supportable is only that a benchmark public since 2024 being compared against models trained later is an uncontrolled variable, which is exactly what most memory-system comparison tables do [495][399]. One further disclosure worth recording as a base rate: at least one memory system paper disclosed post-hoc that source-specific phrases had leaked into its own query-expansion rules, rendering its reported retrieval scores "descriptive development measurements, not independent confirmation", and a third-party analysis attributed a competing system's headline retrieval performance primarily to the default embedding model of its underlying vector store rather than to its architectural contribution [496][400].
